§10.2 The mechanism split — what "confirmed" means here

I sorted every rider by what it actually checks in code, ignoring the label. A strategy is confirmed if the move has to prove itself — the volume-surge thrust-continuations (tw_mnq/mes_thrust_cont, 1.5× volume + still-expanding), the rvol-gated momo (tw_m2k_momo_loose rvol≥1.5, tw_mgc_momo_thrust rvol≥5, tw_us_open_surge_cont rvol≥3), the coil-break with collapsing volume, and the live momentum gate itself (momentum_shadow, with its trend-regime + VWAP-slope-agreement guard). Note tw_m2k_momo_loose is "loose" only in its name — by mechanism it carries an rvol gate, so it sits with the confirmed set, and it made +$163. A strategy is loose if it fires on amplitude alone with no participation check: tw_ride_strength (|net|≥0.30, no volume), tw_mcl_short_with_strength (net≤−1.0, no volume), tw_mym_open_thrust, tw_m2k_open_momentum.

THE FIDELITY TRAP — the loose bleed is worse than it looks

On the optimistic sim P&L the loose family looks harmless — +$66, basically breakeven. On honest fills it's −$1,017. The whole $1,083 gap is fills the rosy sim handed out for free. The single worst offender is tw_mcl_short_with_strength: it prints a gorgeous +$958 on optimistic pnl and a real −$341 — a $1,299 mirage, because shorting a crude down-flush at the perfect 1-ATR fill is a fantasy the spread never honours. And by our own rule, even real_pnl is a floor: live adds force-kills and slippage the replay doesn't model, so the loose wing bleeds more than −$1,017 in the wild, not less.

§10.3 Honest health warnings

This is one week, and these riders were the operator's Monday curve-fit picks that the skeptic already flagged as in-sample — shadowing them is exactly how we find out whether they hold out of sample, and one green week is not that proof. The confirmed number is also top-heavy: strip MNQ out and the other six confirmed contracts total just +$526 on 804 trades — still positive, but modest and easily a week of variance. The win rates are low everywhere (nothing but MNQ clears 50%), so this is a handful of big winners carrying a lot of small losers; a quiet, trendless week would flip the whole thing red. Read "confirmed momentum works" as a directional lean with one good week behind it, not a settled fact. The one number I'd trust more than most is MNQ's confirmed tw_mnq_thrust_cont: +$992 spread across 17 winners from +$17 to +$174, not one lucky trade.

Per-contract verdict. MNQ KEEP confirmed   MGC KEEP confirmed   M2K KEEP confirmed   MES confirmed only   MYM KILL   MBT KILL   MCL KILL   ride-strength family BIN

The Monday read: if momentum ever gets armed off the shadow bench, arm the confirmed version and only on the index-and-gold micros where it paid — MNQ first (it's the whole edge), then MGC, M2K and MES for support. Bin the "ride any strength" family entirely: tw_ride_strength and tw_mcl_short_with_strength are the two biggest bleeders in the whole momentum book, and the second only looks like a winner because the sim fills it at prices crude will never give you. Leave crude, bitcoin and the thin Dow micro out — momentum doesn't pay there even when you make it prove itself.

§11 The exit swap: two pure archetypes, before and after

On Wednesday evening we tore out the old nine-mechanism exit spine on the shadow board and replaced it with two clean rules: a fade (reversion) now gets a 1.5-ATR stop and a 2R target, nothing else; a chase (momentum) now gets a 1-ATR stop and a chandelier that tightens from 3.5 down to 0.5. This section reads the honest money on either side of that switch. The one thing to hold onto up front: the "after" window is short — Wednesday tea-time to Friday close, one regime — so treat everything here as promising, not proven.

THE MARKER

The switch is time-stamped in shadow_exit_archetype_changed_at.json at 2026-07-08 15:47:28 UTC (unix 1783525648), which is about 18:00 in Paris. Every shadow entry before that instant was scored under the old spine; everything after reprices under the two pure archetypes. I split the honest board on entry_ts at that number and never mix the two — the "before" is a dead model. The forward window runs from the first post-switch entry at Wed 15:48 UTC to the last at Fri 21:53 UTC.

BEFORE (old spine)Chase / momentum1,62740.6%-$1,263-$0.78
BEFORE (old spine)Fade / reversion72730.3%-$4,456-$6.13
AFTER (pure archetype)Chase / momentum97335.7%-$2,449-$2.52
AFTER (pure archetype)Fade / reversion2634.6%-$430-$16.54

Two honest cautions before anyone reads a verdict into those per-trade numbers. First, these are not the same trades repriced two ways — the switch is a forward cutover, so "before" and "after" are different entries on different days. The momentum book looks worse per trade after the switch (-$2.52 vs -$0.78), but Wednesday-to-Friday was its own regime and I can't cleanly hand that to the exit change rather than to which entries happened to fire. Second, and this is the big one: the pure reversion exit barely got a forward test at all. Only one strategy — passive_mgc_hours — traded as a fade after the switch, 26 fills, all gold, all a loss. There is no honest forward read on the new 1.5-ATR-and-2R fade rule this week. It just didn't trade.

§11.2 Inside the momentum book: the give-back works, the entries don't

Where the "after" window does have enough trades to say something — the 973 momentum fills — the exit-reason mix is genuinely encouraging, and it tells a clean story. Split those fills by how they died:

How it endedFillsWin%Honest P&LPer tradeAvg hold
CHANDELIER (trail caught it)23456%+$2,373+$10.1412.9 min
TARGET (ran to 2R)55100%+$1,447+$26.318.2 min
SESSION_FLAT (rode to the bell)31152%+$1,014+$3.265.4 min
STOP (1-ATR hit)3730%-$7,283-$19.529.3 min

Read that top-down and the message is loud. Every winning exit route is in the black: the chandelier booked +$2,373 across 234 trades, the 2R target went 55-for-55 for +$1,447, and even the trades that just rode to the session bell added +$1,014. Together the three "let it work" exits made +$4,834. The entire loss of the momentum book — and more — is the stop line: 373 trades, not a single winner, -$7,283. That is not an exit problem. The tightening chandelier is doing exactly its job of protecting and harvesting the trades that go your way; what's bleeding the book is the roughly two-in-five entries that go straight to the 1-ATR stop. This matches what we already believe on the desk — a clean give-back architecture is +EV on momentum, and the real work left is entry quality, not the exit knob.

Post-switch momentum book by exit route (honest P&L). The three green "let it work" bars sum to +$4,834; the single red stop bar is -$7,283. The chandelier is not the leak — the entries that hit the stop are.

§11.3 Is the 2R fade target too far? Yes — but that's a tune, not the fix

We logged a suspicion worth testing: on the reversion side the 2R target sits so far out that fades never reach it — they mostly ride to the session-flat cut instead. The forward evidence agrees emphatically but thinly. Across the whole week the pure 2R target fired exactly twice on fade trades (both wins, +$71 average), and in the post-switch window it fired 2 of 26 times. TARGET is essentially a target nobody hits.

To probe this with more than 26 trades I measured the maximum favourable excursion — how far each fade ever ran in its favour, in ATR units, straight off the 5s bars, which is exit-agnostic (the price path is the same whatever exit rule you bolt on). Across 753 filled fade entries this week:

Fade ever ran at least…TradesShare
1.0 ATR in your favour23130.7%
1.5 ATR15921.1%
2.0 ATR11715.5%
2.5 ATR8711.6%

The median fade never gets past about a third of an ATR in your favour (0.34 ATR); three-quarters of them top out around 1.3 ATR. A 2R target — which sits at two-to-three ATR of favourable run depending on how you count the R — is reached by at most one fade in six, and often fewer. Pull the target in to a single ATR and you'd fire it roughly twice as often (about 31% of trades touch 1 ATR versus 15% touching 2). So yes: the target is too far, and a nearer one would actually book wins instead of leaving them to bleed back to the bell.

HONEST

Before anyone arms a 1R fade target, remember what actually sinks the fade book: not the target you miss on the winners, but the stop you eat on the losers. The fades that got stopped this week lost -$13.18 apiece; the whole reversion archetype runs -$6 to -$16 a trade. Booking the winners at 1R rather than 2R converts a handful of session-flat rides into small green — a genuine improvement — but it does nothing about the fades walking into the stop. It's a sharpening, not a rescue. And it's a sharpening measured on a forward window of 26 fade trades in a single contract over two and a bit days. That is not enough to bank.

§11.4 The plain takeaway

The switch to two pure exits is clean and the marker split is honest, but the forward window is short and one-sided. On momentum the new exits look right: the chandelier, the 2R target and the ride-to-bell all book positive money — every dollar of the loss is in the entries that hit the 1-ATR stop, so keep the exit and go work the entries KEEP. On reversion we learned almost nothing forward — only 26 fade trades fired, all in gold, all red — so the new 1.5-ATR-and-2R rule is effectively untested this week WATCH. The one real signal is that the 2R target is too far: fades rarely run two ATR in your favour, so a nearer target is worth a proper study — but it's a tune on a book whose real bleed is the stop, and 26 forward trades can't confirm it.

§12 The two live gates, their shadow children, and the knife-filter

The live desk fires exactly two entry gates: vwap_dip and vwap_pullback. Both are reversion — they buy weakness and sell strength back toward the session VWAP. A dozen of our shadow sims are just those same two gates with a knob turned or a carve-out bolted on. This week I lined every one of them up on honest fills. The short version: the whole family lost money, the sim badly flattered it, and the one tweak that was supposed to help — only trading the calm tape — made it worse, not better.

§12.1 What the two gates actually are

vwap_pullback — In an established trend (VWAP sloping the right way) that has already had a real impulse move, wait for price to drift back and tag VWAP from the trend side on light volume, then fire with the trend on the first turn bar. The live falling-knife guard skips the tag if volume is heavy (rvol >= pb_max_rvol, live cap 0.6). It is meant to buy the controlled pullback in a trend, not catch a knife. In practice, on a chop week, it spends most of its time fading moves that don't come back.

vwap_dip — The deep one. LONG only when price is stretched a full 4 ATRs below VWAP and two green confirm bars print (SHORT the mirror). It is so selective it fires essentially zero times a week live — it only wakes up for a violent flush. Everything we learn about "the dip" this week comes from the looser dip_loose shadow, which drops the floor to 2.5 ATR and needs only one green bar.

§12.2 How they behaved this week, on money that's real

Here is the honest scoreboard for the two families. Every number below is scored on real_pnl (the tick-repriced fill), with the optimistic sim P&L shown next to it so you can see the gap.

Gate familyTradesSim saysHonest fillsPer tradeWin %Verdict
vwap_pullback (17 variants)1,281+$3,368−$4,754−$3.7137%KILL
vwap_dip (dip_loose)131+$1,200−$184−$1.4153%KILL

Read the two P&L columns as a pair. The pullback family's sim ledger says it made +$3,368 this week; the honest ledger says it lost $4,754. That is an eight-thousand-dollar swing between the fantasy fill and the real one — exactly the 1.1–3.1× downside understatement we keep warning about, and the reason nothing here gets armed on a sim number. The dip is a gentler story only because it barely trades and its win rate sits just above half, but half of a coin-flip that pays fees to play is not an edge.

Left pair = vwap_pullback family, right pair = vwap_dip. Green bar up = what the sim claims, red bar down = honest fills. The gold line is break-even. The sim is looking at a different, kinder market than the one we'd actually trade.

§12.3 Which shadow sims are children of which gate

Almost the entire shadow bench is these two gates in fancy dress. The pullback children all reuse the live _gate_pullback with one thing changed, so there's no logic drift — just the knob under test. Not one of them turned the fade green this week.

Shadow variantThe one changenHonest $/trade
pullback_loosewider VWAP-tag / lower impulse floor165−$542−$3.28
pullback_quietonly fires in quiet tape (rvol≤0.20)97−$422−$4.35
tw_deadtape_standdownstand down only in dead tape256−$960−$3.75
tw_clock_standdownstand down in 4 named UTC hours219−$891−$4.07
tw_strength_ceiling_pbskip the fade when |net_atr|≥0.3036−$267−$7.41
tw_mgc_pb_midband_block / _strong_onlygold trend-band carve-outs38−$306−$8.1
dip_loose DIPdip floor 4.0→2.5 ATR, 1 confirm131−$184−$1.41

The carve-out sims are the telling ones. deadtape_standdown and clock_standdown are the base pullback that abstains in the tape you'd think is worst — and they're the two single biggest bleeders on the bench, because standing down in dead hours doesn't help when the fade loses in the live hours too. The carve-outs trimmed the edges of the wrong thing.

§12.4 The condition filter — and the counter-intuitive part

The dream has always been a favourable-condition gate: learn the tape state where the fade wins, and only let it fire there — stop it walking into the red. The natural first guess was "the fade is a calm-tape trade, so gate it to quiet volume." We have that exact experiment already running as pullback_quiet, which fires the identical live gate but only when rvol ≤ 0.20.

THE FINDING THAT SURPRISED ME

Restricting the fade to the quietest tape made it worse, not better. The all-volume pullback lost −$3.28 a trade and won 47% of the time. The quiet-only version lost −$4.35 a trade and its win rate collapsed to 31%. Calm is not the friend of this fade — it's a trap. In the thinnest tape there's no counter-flow to hand you the mean-reversion, so the drift just keeps drifting and the fade sits in the red the whole hold. A calm-tape band is the wrong filter.

The axis that actually separates the winners from the run-overs is direction, not volume. Split the fades by side: the LONG fades (buying a dip) were nearly break-even at −$0.40 a trade, while the SHORT fades (selling strength back down) lost −$6.06 a trade. And by contract the picture is even starker:

ContractFadesHonest $/trade
MGC (gold)157−$597−$3.80
MES76−$264−$3.48
MBT30−$167−$5.57
MNQ84+$41+$0.49

Gold alone lost $597 across 157 fades and is the single biggest hole in the family. Every fade that leaned against this week's gold move — and gold trended — got run over, exactly the pattern we flagged in the reversion autopsy. Meanwhile MNQ was actually a whisker green at +$0.49 a trade. That matches everything else we know: the fade's only honest niche is MNQ, and the fix is not "wait for calm," it's "don't fade a market that's trending, and above all don't fade gold." A |slope|/direction veto is the shape of the filter; a low-vol band is not.

Honest caveat: this is one chop week and the per-contract counts are small (MNQ's edge is +$41 on 84 trades — a rounding error, not a bankable claim). None of this survives as "arm a filter." What it survives as is a diagnosis: the calm-tape band is falsified, and the direction/trend axis is where the next honest gate has to live.

§12.5 The knife-filter — the obvious bolt-on that doesn't pay

The tempting idea is to bolt the aggressor tape and the order book onto these gates as a confirmation filter — only fade when the flow agrees, bail when it flips. I tested it honestly and it does not pay:

  • As a momentum entry filter, aggressor confirmation was null — the buy/sell imbalance at entry had no predictive relationship with the outcome (p = 0.40). Knowing who was hitting the tape when we fired told us nothing about whether the trade worked.
  • The flow-flip exit — cut the trade the moment aggressor flow reverses — died at tick resolution. By the time the flip is real enough to read, the adverse move has already happened; the exit fires late and books the loss it was meant to dodge.

As for the deeper question — does the L2 order book give the fades a usable veto (absorption at the level, a wall that says "the mean-reversion is here")? That's examined properly in the dedicated L2 order book section against depth.db for the Thu+Fri window, and I'll point you there rather than half-do it here. The headline you can carry over: the book does not hand these fades a clean, usable stop-firing veto this week. The knife-filter, in every form I could test, is a bolt-on that doesn't earn its bolt.

MONDAY TAKEAWAY

Both live gates are reversion and both bled on honest fills this week; the pullback family's sim P&L (+$3,368) is a fiction against its real result (−$4,754), so trust nothing that isn't tick-priced. The calm-tape filter is falsified — quiet tape made the fade worse (win rate 47%→31%). The real axis is direction and contract: gold fades got run over (−$597), MNQ was the only whisker of green. The knife-filter — aggressor confirm and flow-flip exit — is null and does not pay. Next honest gate lives on a slope/direction veto, MNQ-only, not a volume band.

§13 The order book — our first honest look

You caught us on this one. We have been recording the full top-five order book for MNQ, MES and MGC — every resting bid and offer, five levels deep, four times a second — and until this week nobody had opened it. So this is the first time we have actually looked at the book instead of guessing about it. What follows is a first look, not a verdict: it is one-and-a-third days of tape, one market mood, and everything here is in-sample. Read it as "here is what the book seems to be telling us, and here is the one thread worth chasing next week" — not as a filter you can arm on Monday.

§13.1 What we actually captured

The recorder ran from Thursday 09 July 14:17 UTC to Friday 10 July 21:00 UTC. In that window we banked 360,550 book snapshots on MNQ (about 31 hours of near-continuous tape), 196,656 on MES and 188,968 on MGC — all three spanning the same Thu-into-Fri stretch. M2K only came online for the last 1.8 hours, so I have set it aside. Coverage is dense — roughly one snapshot every 250 milliseconds, deduped when nothing moved — with a single thin patch during the 21:00–22:00 UTC settlement break on Thursday (47 snapshots that whole hour instead of ~14,000). MNQ is the deepest and the cleanest, so I lead with it everywhere below.

SET EXPECTATIONS

One-and-a-third days. One regime (a quiet Thursday grind and a news-whippy Friday). In-sample — I am reading the same tape I would trade on. Everything here needs a second and third week across different moods before a single pound rides on it. I will say plainly at the end what "enough" would look like.

§13.2 Does the book point the way? No — if anything it points the wrong way

The obvious hope is that a bid-heavy book means price is about to go up. It does not. I took all 360k MNQ snapshots, measured the book's top-five imbalance at each one, and then looked at where the mid actually was 10, 30 and 60 seconds later. The relationship is essentially nothing — the correlation between imbalance and the next 60-second move is −0.006 (10s: −0.015, 30s: −0.009). Every imbalance bucket coin-flips: price ticked up 48% to 54% of the time no matter how the book was leaning, and the median move in every bucket is sub-tick.

The one place a signal appears, it is backwards. When the book was heavily bid-stacked (imbalance above +0.3 — the reading that screams "support, buyers everywhere") the average next-60-second move was −0.61 points, i.e. slightly down. A stacked bid book was, on average, a mild sell. This is the same weakly-contrarian shrug we got from the aggressor tape: resting size is not a compass. If anything, the fattest, most inviting side of the book is the one most likely to get run over.

MNQ book imbalance at tsnapsavg move next 60swent up %
ask-heavy (< −0.3)6,402+0.48 pt50.6%
−0.3 to −0.175,512+0.22 pt50.0%
flat (−0.1 to +0.1)203,809+0.25 pt50.3%
+0.1 to +0.368,196+0.22 pt50.9%
bid-heavy (> +0.3)5,598−0.61 pt52.9%

So the naive "book points the way" idea is dead on arrival, exactly as it was for the tape. That is worth knowing in itself — it stops us building a gate on a signal that was never there.

§13.3 The knife test: does fading into a wall lose more?

Here is your real question. When our fade and pullback sims buy a dip or sell a rip, are they walking straight into a stacked wall of the opposite side — a pile of sellers overhead when we buy, a pile of buyers underneath when we sell? And when they do, does it hurt more? I pulled every fade-family entry (the pullback, dip and chop sims) on MNQ/MES/MGC that fired inside the depth window — 97 distinct signal moments (122 rows before I collapsed the cases where three sims fired the same bar) — matched each to the book within 2.5 seconds of entry, measured the opposing-side size against the same-side size, and scored on honest real_pnl.

First the blunt fact: all 97 of these fades lost money as a group (−$288 on honest fills across the window). That is the reversion bleed we already know, showing up again. But the book does carve them, and it carves the longs cleanly. A pullback long that bought into an ask stack 1.3× or more the size of the bids below it lost an average of −$17.51 and won only 1 time in 8. The same long into a thin overhead (ask under 0.7× the bid) made +$3.73 on average and won 54%. The gradient is monotone and it makes plain sense: you buy the dip, but there is a wall of sellers overhead, price can't clear them, you get stopped.

Pullback/dip LONGs on MNQ/MES/MGC — average honest P&L by the overhead (ask) book at entry

The catch — and it is a big one — is n=8. Eight long entries into a wall over one-and-a-third days is a story, not a statistic. And the short side actively refuses to play along: the three shorts that sold into a heavy bid wall actually won (+$21 each), the opposite of the veto. So what I have is a clean, sensible, economically-shaped signal on the long side (don't buy a dip into stacked overhead) built on eight trades, and noise on the short side. That is a lead, not a filter.

§13.4 What the book did around the big moves

The marquee move of the window was Friday's 14:32:55 whipsaw — MNQ fell from ~29,956 to ~29,677, about 279 points in 27 seconds, then reversed +120 straight back. This is exactly the kind of move where you would hope the book warns you. It did the opposite. All the way down the first leg the book was heavily bid-stacked — 400 to 650 lots on the bid against ~150–200 on the ask, imbalance pinned around +0.5 — the textbook "support is stacked" picture. Price sliced through it without pausing. Then the offers were simply pulled: the ask stack collapsed from ~226 lots to ~35 in a single snapshot as price gapped 29,902 → 29,850, and moments later the whole book evaporated to single digits on both sides. The last 170 points was a genuine sell avalanche — 5,204 then 5,136 lots sold per five seconds — pouring into an empty book. That is a vacuum, not absorption: nobody ate a wall, the liquidity left and price fell through the hole.

For contrast, Friday's calmer +43.75 rip at 12:13:40 was flow-led: the ask overhead was ordinary and thin (~25 lots, never stacked), and a one-sided buy sweep of 2,571 lots against just 54 sells lifted every offer faster than it could be replaced. Different mechanism, same lesson — the big directional moves happened where the far side was thin, whether it was pulled (the crash) or simply lifted (the rip). In neither case did a stacked opposing wall stand in the way; in the crash, the stacked side gave a flat-out false "support" reading right before the worst drop of the week.

THE ONE THING

Resting size is not a promise. The fattest side of the book is where the trap is: a stacked bid book preceded the biggest sell-off in the sample, and the fastest moves ran through vacuums, not walls.

§13.5 Honest takeaway

Two things survive this first look. One is settled: K book imbalance is not a direction signal — it is a flat-to-contrarian coin flip, same as the tape, so we will not build a "lean with the book" gate. The other is a lead worth one more look: T a pullback long into a stacked ask wall is a bad trade — seven of eight lost, and it makes mechanical sense. But eight trades on one-and-a-third in-sample days is nowhere near arm-able, and the short-side mirror didn't hold, so I am flagging it, not shipping it.

What would make it real: keep the depth recorder running so we bank the book across a few different weeks — a trend day, a chop day, a data day — and let the fade sims accumulate. I want to see this wall-veto on the order of 30–50 long entries per regime, with the short side either confirming or being explicitly left out, before it becomes a live knife-veto. The recorder is cheap and already running; the only thing between us and an honest answer is a few more weeks of tape. For this week: nothing to arm, one clear thread to chase, and the naive "book points the way" idea taken off the table for good.

Monday takeaway: Don't trust the book to point direction — it's a contrarian coin flip. The one real thread is that buying a dip into a stacked wall of overhead sellers loses hard, but it's eight trades on 1.3 days, so keep the recorder running and let it prove itself over a few more weeks before we veto anything.

✓ A skeptic re-ran the money claim — it HELD
REAL. On honest net real_pnl for Mon6-Fri10 Jul (n=3797, desk -$8599): rvol<0.8 = 58.9% of entries (claim says two-thirds; actually ~three-fifths) and -$8414 = 97.9% of the net loss; muzzling it desk-wide lifts the desk to -$185, a +$8414 (~£6.6k) prize — larger than the £3.5k claimed, so the claim is conservative. Robust: loses all 5 days, avg pnl monotone in rvol, net of fees, and a shadow floor (understates live). One honest caveat — the desk is net -EV so part of the prize is trade-less: a random 59% cut would leave -$3533 vs -$185, so genuine signal selectivity beyond trading-less is ~$3.3k (~£2.6k / ~$3.5k), which is exactly where £3500 lands. Per-sim confirmed exactly: passive_chop is -EV in every rvol bucket (kill it, no pocket); momentum_shadow flips positive above rvol 0.8 (0.8-1.5 +$1.63/trade, 1.5+ +$3.82/trade) — a real switch-into pocket. Not curve-fit, not thin-n, not cost-blind; only nuance is the -EV desk making it partly trade-less.

§14 The OFF switch: when should each sim NOT fire?

The whole prize this week is not turning a sim on. It is muzzling one on the days its edge is not in the room. So I stopped asking "which sim is good" and asked the only question that pays: given the tape I can read at the moment of entry — how fast it is moving (atr%), how much volume is showing up (rvol), what session we are in — when should the trigger simply be dead? The honest answer, on this week's real fills, is boring and strong: the desk keeps firing into dead tape, and dead tape is where essentially all the money goes.

READ THE TWO TRAPS FIRST

Trap 1 — the daily lottery. I run ~20 to 28 sims against 7 contracts every day. On any given day a handful come out green by chance. Look at who "won" each day this week, best sim by real P&L with a real sample: Mon it was tw_mnq_thrust_cont (+$432), Tue momentum_shadow (+$238), Wed tw_mgc_coil_break_long (+$355), Thu dip_loose (+$69), Fri tw_mgc_pb_midband_block (+$58). Five days, five different winners, no repeat. Chasing yesterday's hero is chasing noise. And it is not even a close race for green: on Fri only 3 of 21 sims finished positive, on Tue 4 of 22. A switch built on "who won lately" is a switch built on a coin that lands somewhere new every morning.

Trap 2 — the switch has to be REACTIVE, not a forecast. We have proven to death that regime is unpredictable at the minute scale, so the switch cannot say "today will be trending." It can only read the condition that is present right now. And the labelled regime confirms why: bucket every trade by the session_regime tag and both big buckets bleed — "chop" lost $4,059 over 1,013 trades and "trending" lost $1,147 over 1,203. The label does not separate winners from losers. A live number read off the tape at entry does. So every rule below is a present-tense reading, never a prediction.

§14.1 The cheap desk-wide gate: is the tape tradeable at all?

Before tuning any single sim, ask the cheapest possible question — one boundary that muzzles the whole desk when the tape is not worth trading. I cut every entry this week (the ~2,277 with a clean condition read; Monday morning has a funnel-capture gap, so it is under-counted, not wrong) by relative volume at entry. The shape is stark:

rvol band at entrytradesnet real P&Lwin%
< 0.8  (thin / quiet)1,566−$5,93237%
0.8 – 1.2288+$9144%
1.2 – 2.0268+$78737%
≥ 2.0  (blow-off)155−$77228%

Sixty-nine percent of everything the desk does happens in thin tape (rvol under 0.8), and that one bucket carries essentially the entire loss. Put a floor at rvol 0.8 and the picture flips: the 711 trades above the floor net roughly breakeven (+$106), while the 1,566 below it lost $5,932. The floor does not make money — it stops a guaranteed bleed. That is the honest framing, and it is the single most robust thing in this section: split the week day by day and the below-floor bucket is negative on all five days (−$1,118, −$860, −$553, −$1,488, −$1,913). There is no lucky day where firing into dead tape worked.

Two guards on the claim. First, it is not just the passive_chop reversion book (which is dead anyway — see below): strip passive_chop out entirely and the below-0.8 bucket still bled −$5,547 across 1,459 trades while the in-band trades made +$1,257. The bleed is desk-wide, not one bad sim. Second, the upper cut (drop rvol ≥ 2, the blow-off bar) adds a bit more but rests on only 155 trades — treat the floor as bankable and the ceiling as a lean.

THE DESK-WIDE MUZZLE

OFF for every sim when rvol at entry is under 0.8. It costs ~$106/wk of give-up and it stopped ~$5,900/wk of shadow losses this week, negative every single day out-of-gate. It is a floor that says "the tape isn't paying today," not a money-maker. KEEP

§14.2 Per-sim pockets: where each big one actually lives

The desk gate is blunt. Now the sharper cut — for the three biggest sims, isolate the pocket where that sim makes its money from the tape you can see at entry, and state the muzzle.

§14.3 momentum_shadow — a real amplitude plateau FLOOR IT

This is the one sim with an honest, structured pocket. Slice its 296 conditioned trades by atr% at entry and the edge is not a lucky spike, it is a plateau: below atr% 0.045 it loses, above it wins, and it wins in three consecutive bands.

atr% at entrytradesnet realwin%
< 0.0394−$17136%
0.03 – 0.04561−$11343%
0.045 – 0.0661+$28641%
0.06 – 0.0835+$23246%
≥ 0.0845+$22942%

Three green bands in a row is what a real edge looks like — a knife-edge would be one lucky band with losers either side. In plain terms: this sim needs the bar to actually be moving. Give it a dead, low-amplitude tape and it buys a twitch that goes nowhere and pays the spread. Add one more guard — it also gives money back when volume goes vertical (rvol ≥ 2 lost −$168 at a 22% win rate, the classic buy-the-top). Put both together, muzzle unless atr% ≥ 0.045 and rvol < 2, and the pocket holds +$795 over 113 trades (47% win) while the muzzled remainder was −$331. The floor keeps every dollar of the profit and throws away a losing tail. And it holds up day by day — the pocket was positive or flat on 4 of 5 days, its one red day was a rounding error (−$14). This matches the amplitude-floor thesis already deployed observe-only; the data here says keep it and let it graduate.

§14.4 passive_chop — no pocket exists KILL

The desk's biggest sim by volume (1,088 trades) and its biggest loser (−$4,249 real). I went looking for its pocket the same way, and there isn't one. Every atr% band loses. Every rvol band loses. There is no corner of the tape where fading the chop paid this week:

slicetradesnet real
atr% < 0.0344−$128
atr% 0.045–0.0638−$150
atr% ≥ 0.0886−$447
rvol < 0.8186−$686
rvol ≥ 1.223−$89

When a sim loses in every bucket, there is no OFF switch that saves it — the OFF switch is the sim. This is the reversion-autopsy verdict landing again from a different angle: passive_chop fades trends and gets run over, and no observable-at-entry condition tells you which fade will be the one that keeps going. The muzzle here is permanent.

§14.5 pullback_loose — same thin-tape disease FLOOR IT

Down −$542 on the week, and the story is the desk-wide story in miniature: it is a thin-tape bleeder. Below rvol 0.8 it lost −$703 over 127 trades; at rvol 0.8 and above it made +$147 over just 27. The rvol floor is the right muzzle again — but be honest, 27 in-gate trades is too thin to bank on its own. Read it as one more vote for the desk-wide floor rather than a standalone edge.

§14.6 Plateau or knife-edge? — the one-week honesty check

Everything here is one week, in-sample, on shadow fills. So which of these is a robust plateau I would defend, and which is a shape I fit to five days of noise?

Plateau (defend it): the desk-wide rvol<0.8 muzzle. It is negative every single day, holds with passive_chop removed, and rests on 1,566 trades. That is not a fit, that is a floor.

Plateau-ish (lean into it): momentum_shadow's atr% ≥ 0.045 amplitude floor. Three adjacent green bands and positive 4 of 5 days is genuinely plateau-shaped, but two big days carry most of the dollars — graduate it observe-first, don't size it yet.

Too thin to bank: pullback_loose's floor (27 trades) and momentum's rvol≥2 ceiling (155 trades). Directionally right, evidentially thin. Leans, not laws.

Takeaway, plain: the money isn't in a clever new trigger, it's in a mute button. Two-thirds of what this desk does happens in tape too thin to pay, and that thin tape is where all the loss lives — every day, this week. Wire one desk-wide floor: no sim fires when rvol is under 0.8. Then give momentum_shadow its own amplitude floor (atr% ≥ 0.045) and retire passive_chop, which has no favourable pocket to switch into. And remember why the winner's list rotates every morning — the switch reads the tape that is in the room, it never predicts the day.

§15 Greenfield: if we can't catch the big misses, what would?

Every week the tape throws a handful of big, clean runs and every sim on the desk sits and watches them go by. This chapter starts from those misses, tries to build ONE brand-new signal from scratch that would have caught them, and then does the only thing that matters — trades it on honest fills, net of costs, and checks whether it survives outside the one week I fit it on. The short version: it doesn't. But the reason it doesn't is worth more than a working curve-fit would have been.

§15.1 The misses, laid out honestly

I ran the full missed-run census on MNQ (the contract with the deepest tape and book coverage). In the last seven days there were 76 runs of 1.5×ATR or more — a 15-minute move of at least 73.9 points, against a typical 15-minute range of 49.2. Of those 76, the desk sat out roughly 59. That is not a rounding error; that is the whole game walking past us. The census sorts them into four cause-clusters:

Cause clusterRunsSat outWhat it looks like
VACUUM (moved against the tape)3627price ran the opposite way to the aggressors
FLOW-LED (aggressors drove it)1915heavy one-sided lifting/hitting, then the move
OPEN / NEWS spike181513:00–15:00 UTC cash-open & data prints
UNCLASSIFIED (quiet / no tape)32drifted with no fingerprint

The seductive one is FLOW-LED. Nineteen runs where the aggressor tape leaned hard one way and then price went that way, and we sat out fifteen of them. The obvious greenfield hypothesis writes itself: build a tape-ignition entry — when net aggressor flow floods one way, jump in that direction and ride. That is the signal I set out to build. It is also completely wrong, and the data says so in the first test.

§15.2 The first test kills the obvious idea

Before building any trade logic I asked the plainest possible question: across every 5-second bar this week, if I know the net aggressor flow over the previous 60 seconds, does that tell me anything about where price goes next? I bucketed all 80,352 MNQ bars by their trailing-60s net flow and measured the average move over the next three minutes.

Mean 3-minute forward move (points) by trailing-60s net aggressor flow, MNQ, all bars this week. Left = heaviest net selling, right = heaviest net buying.

Read that curve. When the tape is flooded with selling, price drifts up over the next three minutes (+3.70 points in the heaviest-selling bucket). When the tape is flooded with buying, price drifts down (−0.84). The overall correlation between flow and forward return is −0.065 — small, but consistently negative. Aggressive flow on this tape is contrarian: the people crossing the spread are, on average, the ones getting run over. Those FLOW-LED runs in the census are survivorship — the census finds the handful of times flow and move happened to agree, but across the whole week flow points the wrong way. A tape-chasing momentum entry would have been structurally backwards.

THE ONE DURABLE LESSON

Do not build a "follow the aggressor tape" momentum entry on these contracts. Net aggressor flow is a weak fade signal, not a chase signal. Every instinct that says "the buyers are in control, go with them" is, this week, exactly inverted.

§15.3 So I flipped it — and built the fade instead

If flow is contrarian, the honest greenfield signal is the opposite of the obvious one: seller-exhaustion fade — when the 60-second net flow shows heavy net selling, go long the capitulation and hold for the bounce. I built it as a real trade: enter at the next bar's open, no overlapping positions, hold to a time cap, and charge the honest cost. For the cost I didn't invent a number — I used the desk's own measured gap between optimistic sim P&L and honest fills, which on MNQ is $5.19 per round trip. Baseline drift this week was essentially zero (+0.016pt over 60s), so there's no free long hiding in a rising market — anything the signal earns, it earns.

Seller-exhaustion fade (LONG)TradesNet $$/tradeWin%
flow ≤ −400, hold 5min, +$5 cost300−110−0.453
flow ≤ −600, hold 5min, +$5 cost190+396+2.153
flow ≤ −800, hold 5min, +$5 cost126+452+3.652
… same, but with a 25pt stop / 40pt target300−895−3.046

There is a pulse — but look at where it lives. The signal only clears its own costs at the extreme tail, where the tape has dumped 800+ net contracts in a minute, and only if I hold the full five minutes with no stop, wearing the entire drawdown. The best cell earns $3.6 a trade across 126 trades — and I found it by sweeping thresholds (400/600/800) and horizons (3min/5min) and picking the winner. That is the textbook shape of a curve-fit: nothing at the sensible settings, a sliver at the extreme, and it evaporates the moment you add a realistic stop (the fade walks into more selling before the bounce and gets stopped, turning +$3.6 into −$3.0). Per day it's incoherent too — it loses on three of the five days at the 400 threshold.

§15.4 The out-of-sample test buries it

The only honest way to judge a one-week filter is to point it at data it wasn't fitted on. I don't have next week, but I have two sister contracts with full-week tape — MES and MGC — running the exact same physics the same week. If seller-exhaustion is a real market truth, it should show something there.

Same LONG fade, hold 5min, +$5 costTrades$/tradeWin%
MES · flow ≤ −200331−4.538
MES · flow ≤ −600108−1.746
MGC · flow ≤ −20044−8.045

It loses at every threshold on both. The marginal MNQ win doesn't replicate one contract over, in the same week, on the same idea. That's not an edge with a tuning problem — that's a one-contract, one-week artifact.

§15.5 And the order book had nothing to add

The right instrument for "seller exhaustion" isn't the trade tape at all — it's the L2 book: heavy selling hitting a bid that refuses to drop is genuine absorption. I have 360,550 MNQ depth snapshots across Thursday and Friday, so I checked whether book imbalance (top-of-book and top-3 depth) predicts the next 60–180 seconds. Correlation with forward return: −0.006 to +0.001 — indistinguishable from zero. Bid-heavy and ask-heavy books produced almost identical small forward moves (just the two-day drift). The book, over the window we captured, carries no directional edge to build on either.

§15.6 The plain takeaway

I tried to invent the signal that catches the misses, and the honest answer is that there isn't one hiding in what we capture. The big runs split into three kinds we can now name: news/open spikes (13:00–15:00 UTC — unpredictable from order flow by definition, and a third of the census), contrarian overshoots that snap back faster than our costs can be paid, and a residue of quiet drifts with no fingerprint at all. The one candidate with a heartbeat — fading heavy selling — is a swept-in curve-fit that dies on MES and MGC the same week. KILL the tape-ignition idea, KILL the seller-exhaustion fade, KILL the book-imbalance entry. Nothing goes to the shadow book.

What we keep is the negative knowledge, and it's the most bankable thing in the chapter: the aggressor tape on these contracts is a fade, not a chase. That's a permanent guardrail against the entire family of "follow the flow" momentum entries someone will inevitably want to build next. The misses stay missed — but now we know they're missed because they're genuinely silent, not because we haven't looked.

§16 The Sizing Ladder — Has Any Sim Earned More Size?

Size is earned, not chosen. The ladder is deliberately slow: a sim starts as a shadow at one micro, and only climbs to live-at-1, then 2, then 3 once it has cleared every rung — 8 to 12 weeks of tape, 100 to 200 trades, a positive edge that holds across regimes, a survivable drawdown, a clean reward-to-risk, and a low correlation to the live desk we already run. This week's honest answer to "who's ready?" is short: nobody yet. But the queue is forming, and it's the momentum names standing in it.

§16.1 The green sims built real money this week

Scored on honest fills, eight sims finished the week in the black, and every one of them is a momentum or breakout idea — buying strength, not fading it. The standout is tw_mnq_thrust_cont: 28 trades, +$992 real, a 61% hit rate, and an average winner of $80 against an average loser of $33. That's a genuine 2.4-to-1 payoff — the cleanest reward-to-risk on the whole board. Behind it, the workhorse momentum_shadow put up +$749 across 413 trades, and orb_shadow, the two MGC breakout books, the M2K momentum sim and the MES/MYM thrust books all pitched in.

Here is the winners' book adding up across the week — a real climb to +$2,596 by Thursday, then a small give-back into Friday. That last flat leg is the whole argument for the ladder in one picture.

§16.2 Now score them against the ladder — and the ladder says "not yet"

The trouble isn't the money, it's the calendar. The oldest sims in this list, momentum_shadow and orb_shadow, were born on 3 July. The rest — every tw_ book — started this Monday. So the single most important gate, 8 to 12 weeks of tape, is failed by all of them by a mile. One week is not a track record; it's an anecdote. Against the gates:

tw_mnq_thrust_cont is the clear front-runner and has genuinely earned its place on the first rung of the ladder — keep it as a shadow, keep watching. What it has not earned is a live micro. It has 28 trades, not 100, and three days of tape, not eight weeks. And 44% of its +$992 came from just three trades; strip those and it's still green but far less imposing. That's not a knock — it's exactly why the trade-count gate exists, so one good Monday can't buy its way onto the desk.

THE FEATURE THAT LOOKS LIKE A DELAY

The selective momentum sims trade rarely — tw_mnq_thrust_cont fired 28 times this week, tw_mes_thrust_cont 26, tw_mym_open_thrust just 12. At that pace they'll take months, not weeks, to reach 100–200 trades. That is the discipline working, not a problem to engineer away. A sim that only bets when the tape is really moving should spend most of its life waiting. We let it wait.

§16.3 Two robustness caveats before anyone gets excited

First, the regime warning. momentum_shadow made +$419 on Monday and +$238 on Tuesday when the tape trended, then bled −$8 Thursday and −$42 Friday as it chopped. That's the signature of a strategy that's long the trend and gives it back when the trend stops — it needs to prove it can survive a genuinely dead week before it climbs. Second, tw_mgc_coil_break_long is almost entirely one day: +$355 on Wednesday against −$106, −$22 and −$140 on the other days. Take Wednesday out and it's a bleeder. A single lucky breakout is not an edge, and the ladder's job is to make it prove that over 8 weeks.

§16.4 The Departure Lounge

The other end of the board is easier to read. These are the sims that lost real money by fading strength and standing in front of moves, and they should be nominated for retirement. The report nominates; the operator arms — nothing here is switched off by this page.

SimTradesReal P&LWin%What it does wrong
passive_chop1088−$4,24917%Fades chop across everything (heaviest on MBT); wins 1 in 6RETIRE

tw_ride_strength198−$62738%"Rides strength" but keeps getting run over, worst −$98RETIRE

The pattern is one sentence long: every fade book lost, every momentum book that survived made money. passive_chop alone gave back $4,249 — more than all eight green sims made put together — by selling into moves 1,088 times and winning one trade in six. tw_mbt_fade_euphoria's 6% win rate is the purest version of the mistake: it stood in front of a running crypto tape and got flattened 47 times out of 50. These aren't tuning candidates; they're the wrong trade.

PLAIN TAKEAWAY

No sim has earned a live micro yet — not one clears the 8-to-12-week gate, and the best of them, tw_mnq_thrust_cont, has 28 trades where it needs 100 to 200. Keep the eight momentum winners on the shadow rung and let them accumulate the weeks; their slow trade pace is a feature, so give them until the autumn, not this month. Meanwhile the Departure Lounge — led by passive_chop and the whole fade-and-standdown family — is nominated for retirement, because a book that fades strength has now proved across a thousand trades that it just hands the money back. The report nominates; you arm.

§18 The MNQ thrust-rider: it caught the runs and the stops were free

This one worked. On the honest tick-repriced fills tw_mnq_thrust_cont made $991.6 over 28 trades this week at 61% win — and it did something a shadow almost never does: it beat its own perfect-fill ceiling of $722.9. Real money came in above the fantasy. It buys an MNQ thrust that keeps going, rides it on the ratchet, and when it's wrong the stop costs almost nothing.

§18.1 Why it works: big winners on the ratchet, free losers on the stop

Split the week by how each trade left and the whole story is right there. The ten ratchet exits made $738.3 at 90% win — those are the thrusts that ran and the trailing ratchet let them run. Five more came out on a VWAP exit for another $274.9 at 80%. Against that, the twelve stop-outs — nearly half the book — cost the desk a grand total of $7.9. That's the engine: the exit spine cuts a wrong thrust to basically zero, so the sim only needs its runners to pay and they do.

§18.2 What separates its winners from its losers: bar size

Now the entry conditions. Fair warning on the data first: the documented funnel join (cycle_ts = entry_sid) returns nothing for this sim because entry_sid here is a session bucket, not a cycle timestamp — so I matched each trade to the nearest funnel snapshot in the 60 seconds before entry. That covers 18 of the 28 trades; the other ten have no readable conditions and can't be gated. On the 18 I can see, the cleanest divider by a mile is bar amplitude, atr_pct: winners fired on fatter bars (avg 0.056) than losers (0.047). Spread pointed the same way, softer (winners 0.58 vs losers 0.89) — nobody home, wider fill — but atr_pct is the sharp axis.

THE ONE THING FOR MONDAY

Put an amplitude floor on it: only fire the MNQ thrust when the bar is fat, atr_pct ≥ 0.05. On the 18 trades I can read, that floor is a clean cut — the fat-bar bucket made $510.9 over 9 trades at 67% win, while the thin-bar bucket churned 9 trades to -$4.1 at 33% win.

Bucket (18 funnel-matched)nreal_pnlwin%

§18.3 The honest size of the win

Be straight about what that gate buys. The win-rate split is real and it lines up with the amplitude floor we already run on the other momentum shadows (atr_pct≥0.04). But the money swing is tiny: the thin-bar bucket only lost $4.1, because — as the exit table showed — this sim's losers are already nearly free. So the floor isn't "make more money", it's "make the same money on half the trades with none of the dead-weight churn". That's a cleaner, tighter book, not a richer one. On one week and 18 readable trades this is thin, and like most single-week filters it mostly just means trade less — but here trading less costs you nothing and the thing you cut never won.

Verdict: ARM / SIZE atr_pct≥0.05 floor — it made real money, beat its ceiling, and its exit spine already does the hard job. Give it the amplitude floor and put it on the promotion ladder.

§19 Momentum Shadow: it made money by letting winners run and getting stopped fast

The momentum sim made $748.70 this week on the honest tick-repriced fills, over 413 trades, winning just 42% of them. That's the truth from the join, not the sim's own optimistic ledger. It made money the way a momentum book is supposed to: a third of its exits ratchet up big, and the losers get cut at the stop before they hurt. It left a lot on the table versus a perfect fill ($2,110 ceiling), and almost all of that gap is one contract behaving badly. Read on for the one thing.

$748.70

win rate — wins are big, losses small
$1,361.80

§19.1 Did it make money — yes, and here's exactly how

I re-ran the join myself. Real_pnl summed to $748.70 over 413 scored trades against a perfect-fill ceiling of $2,110.50. So it captured about 35 cents of every ceiling dollar. The engine is dead simple and it's healthy: when a trade works it rides the ratchet, and when it doesn't it eats the stop. Look at the exit mix — the RATCHET exits made $2,375.80 across 131 trades and won 74% of the time, while the STOP exits bled -$1,810.30 across 173 trades and won only 15%. That's not a bug. That's the shape you want: small frequent losers paid for by fewer, fatter winners.

One thing worth flagging: the shorts carried the week. Short trades made $594.80, longs only $153.90, on near-identical trade counts (202 vs 211). And the best contract by a mile was MNQ at +$494.70 over 65 trades — which actually beat its own perfect-fill ceiling of $391.60, meaning the fills it got were clean. Compare that to MGC: a $900 ceiling, but only -$10.30 in real money, because it bled an average of $20.69 per trade to slippage. MGC is where the fill quality falls apart, not the signal.

Exit reasonreal_pnlnwin%
RATCHET (winner runs)$2,375.8013174.0
VWAP_LOSS$237.003360.6
EOD flatten-$53.907639.5
STOP (loser cut)-$1,810.3017315.0

§19.2 Why it wins vs loses — the bar has to be moving

Heads up on the evidence: the entry-condition funnel (rvol, ofi, book imbalance) only matched 9 of the 413 trades this week — 2% coverage. That's too thin to say anything about the order book. So I don't guess; I use the one condition the sim records on every single trade: how fat the bar was when it fired, measured as ATR as a percent of price (entry_atr_abs / entry_price).

And that split is clean. When the bar was dead-thin — under 0.04% ATR — the sim fired 201 times and made almost nothing: $63.40, an average of 32 cents a trade, and it got stopped out half the time (50% stop rate). When the bar had real amplitude — 0.04% ATR or more — it fired 212 times and made $685.30, an average of $3.23 a trade, with the stop rate dropping to 34%. Same signal, but on a thin tape the momentum isn't real, the move dies, and the stop takes you out. This is the amplitude-floor story the desk already half-believes, showing up again in this sim's own numbers.

Average honest $/trade by bar amplitude — thin <0.04% ($0.32) · mid 0.04–0.08% ($2.74) · wide 0.08–0.15% ($5.12) · fat >0.15% ($2.86). The floor is at the first red bar.

§19.3 The one thing for Monday: put a floor under the bar

GATE: only fire when atr_pct >= 0.04%

Stand the sim down when the bar is too thin to carry a move. Below is the split on its own trades. The honest part first: this is mostly a "trade less, trade cleaner" move, not a money geyser. Cutting the thin bucket only adds about $63 in-sample, because those thin trades weren't losing outright — they were just dead weight. But it halves the trade count, lifts the average from 32 cents to $3.23 a trade, and drops the stop-out rate from 50% to 34%. That's a much healthier book firing far fewer shots for essentially the same money — and it lines up with the amplitude floor already armed observe-only on the live momentum shadows.

HONEST CAVEAT

§20 The Breakout Sim Pays — But Two Symbols Are Eating a Third of It

orb_shadow made real money this week: +$195.90 on the honest tick-repriced fills, across 144 trades, winning just 35% of them. §20.1 What it's actually doing: let the runner run, cut the rest

This is a clean breakout book and the exit tape proves it. The money is made entirely by the ratchet: 52 trades trailed out under RATCHET for +$1,042, winning 58% of the time. Against that, 53 trades hit their STOP for -$863, winning basically never (4%). The 38 that timed out at the end of day were a wash (-$3.50). So the shape is simple and healthy — a third of the trades catch a move and get milked, half get stopped for a small controlled loss, and the survivors outrun the stops. That's exactly what you want a breakout to look like, and it's why it's green on a 35% hit rate.

The funnel conditions are no help here — only 7 of 144 trades matched a fut_signal_funnel row, so I'm not going to pretend an rvol or book-imbalance gate is proven on this sim. It isn't. The honest signal this week lives in the sim's own trade log, and it's loud: it's a symbol problem, not a condition problem.

When the mechanism that makes your money is itself losing on a symbol, the symbol doesn't belong in the book. That matches what we already know about crude — it's a different beast, event-driven (OPEC/EIA), and the spread eats the edge. The breakout structure just doesn't hold there.

GATE IT — one filter fixes it · $+163 this week on honest fills.

§21 M2K loose-momentum: a winner that keeps chasing spikes it shouldn't

This one made money this week — $163 honest, on tick-repriced fills, over 76 trades at a 41% win rate. That's the real number, and it beats nothing to fantasy: the perfect-fill ceiling was $358, so slippage ate more than half the paper edge. But the money is real and it's positive. The job here isn't to save it — it's to stop it handing back the gains it's already earning.

$163
honest real_pnl, 76 trades
41%
win rate
$358
perfect-fill ceiling (fantasy)
$311
from ratchet exits alone

§21.1 Did it make money — yes, and it's a pure momentum shape

Score it on the honest fills and it's clean: +$163.4 over 76 trades, 41% win. The shape is textbook loose momentum — a handful of big trailed winners paying for a lot of small stops. Its 29 ratchet exits made +$311 at an 83% win rate; its 37 stop-outs cost -$214 and won just 5% of the time. Both sides trade roughly evenly (44 longs +$92, 32 shorts +$71), so there's no directional bias to fix. When it catches a run it trails it well. The leak is which breakouts it agrees to buy.

§21.2 Why it wins and why it bleeds — it's late to the hot ones

Pull the entry conditions from the funnel (I matched 51 of the 76 trades to a signal-funnel row within 8 seconds — 67% coverage; the other 25 have no funnel row and I can't see their conditions). The split is loud. On the trades it wins, average rvol at entry is 1.01 — normal, calm tape. On the trades it loses, average rvol is 1.68 — the tape is already hot when it fires.

In plain words: this is a breakout buyer, and it's best when the tape is quiet and the move is just starting. When volume is already spiking, the crowd is in ahead of it — it buys a rip that's already spent, and gets run over. The exit reasons prove it. In calm tape (rvol under 1.3) its ratchets locked $252 across 17 trades and its stops cost only -$33. In hot tape (rvol 1.3+) it's the opposite: 16 stop-outs for -$132, and the ratchet rescued just $44. Same strategy, same week — the only thing that changed is it showed up late.

THE BEHAVIOUR IN ONE LINE

Quiet tape, it's early and the ratchet trails a real move to a locked win. Hot tape, it's late — it chases a spike the crowd already rode, and eats the stop.

§21.3 The one thing for Monday — stand down when rvol is hot

The single cleanest cut is a volume gate: only fire when rvol is below 1.3. On the 51 trades I can see conditions for, that one split does all the work.

BucketTradesReal P&LWin %What it is
Calm tape — rvol < 1.326+$279.462%early on a fresh move; ratchet trails it
Hot tape — rvol ≥ 1.325-$88.528%late to a spent spike; stops out

The hot-rvol half is -$88.5 across 25 trades at a 28% win rate — it's not marginal, it's a loss-maker outright. Veto it and the visible book climbs from +$190.9 to +$279.4, an in-sample swing of about +$88 for the week (roughly £70), while cutting the trade count in half.

Real P&L by entry-rvol bucket (matched trades). Left to right: rvol <0.8 (+$135), 0.8–1.2 (+$22), 1.2–1.6 (+$79), 1.6–2.2 (-$13), ≥2.2 (-$32). Gold line marks the rvol=1.3 gate — everything green sits left of it, both red bars sit right.

§21.4 The honest caveat

This is one week and in-sample, and the gate only speaks to the 51 trades that had a funnel row — a third of the book is dark. And like most single filters, this one mostly just means trade less: it doesn't turn losers into winners, it declines to take them. But the signal here is unusually clean and it has a real mechanism behind it (late-to-the-spike, not a stat fluke), the same "amplitude/tape floor" logic already deployed on the other momentum shadows. Don't size this yet — it's not proven across regimes. Arm the rvol<1.3 gate observe-only alongside the others and let it forward-validate.

§22 MGC coil-break: it pays, but only when the break has already left home

This one made money this week. On the honest tick-repriced fills, tw_mgc_coil_break_long booked +$136.92 over 134 trades at a 36% win rate. Small, but green — and it did it while its perfect-fill ceiling was $712, so most of the fantasy got eaten by slippage. The real story is simple: when it fires on a break that's already pulled away from VWAP, it runs; when it fires right on top of VWAP, it's just buying fake breakouts in chop and feeding the stop.

+$136.92
real_pnl, 134 trades
36%
win rate
$712
perfect-fill ceiling (fantasy)
+$380

§22.1 What it's actually doing

It's a coil-break long on gold. The shape of the week is textbook trend-follower: a small number of big winners carry a big pile of small losers. All 48 of its green trades exited on the RATCHET (the trailing lock) for +$2,010. The 74 losers all died on the STOP for -$1,347. Ten more scratched out on a VWAP exit for a token +$72. So the machinery works — winners are allowed to run, losers get cut — and it nets positive by a whisker.

The question is which breaks are real. I pulled the entry conditions off the signal funnel for every trade (134/134 matched — full coverage, using the last funnel tick before each fire). ATR, RVOL, OFI and spread barely move between winners and losers — they're not the tell. The one condition that separates them is how far price already was from VWAP at entry. Winners fired at an average VWAP distance of 0.053%; losers at 0.033%. In plain words: the trades that worked had already broken out and moved. The trades that bled were fired right on the VWAP, on a coil that hadn't actually committed yet — a fake break in the chop.

THE PATTERN

Fire on top of VWAP and you're guessing the break — you get 31% winners and a slow bleed. Wait until the break has already left home (VWAP distance ≥ 0.04%) and the same ratchet catches real runs — 42% winners, and the winners are 2.5× bigger ($1,025 of ratchet gains vs $410 on the near-VWAP side).

§22.2 The one thing for Monday: only fire when the break has left VWAP

Split its own 134 trades by VWAP distance at entry. The line at 0.04% cleaves the winners from the losers cleanly:

0.02 – 0.04%13-$94.5623%-$7.27chop

≥ 0.06% (well clear)46+$204.4839%+$4.45real break

Real_pnl by VWAP distance at entry. Red = fired on the VWAP (chop). Green = fired after the break already moved.

The gate: only take the coil break when VWAP distance ≥ 0.04% at entry. On this week's own trades that keeps 57 trades for +$380.16 (42% win, +$6.67/trade) and throws away 77 trades that netted -$243.24 (31% win, -$3.16/trade). Same ratchet, same stop — you just stop taking the un-committed breaks. In-sample money swing this week: +$243, i.e. from +$137 to +$380 on the identical machinery.

HONEST CAVEAT

This is one week, 134 trades, in-sample. The gate cuts 57% of the volume, so part of what you're seeing is just "trade less in the chop" — but it's not only that: per-trade EV genuinely flips from -$3.16 to +$6.67 and win rate climbs 31%→42%, and there's a real mechanism (an extended break is a confirmed break). I'd want it to hold up on a second week before sizing. For now: GATE vwap_dist ≥ 0.04% in observe-only, and keep it running — it's already green.

Verdict: KEEP it — it makes money as-is — and add the VWAP-distance gate to see if next week confirms the +$243 swing before this earns real size.

§23 MGC Momentum-Thrust: it made money, but the whole week is six trades

This little MGC thrust sim made $126.9 on the honest fills this week over 9 scored rows, 56% of them green. That's a real profit, and it landed on the right side of the tape. But before you get excited: those 9 rows are exit-variants of just 6 actual entry decisions, and almost every dollar came from three gold shorts on Monday morning. §23.1 1. The money — it's real, and it's almost all short

I re-ran the join myself. Nine scored rows, real_pnl summing to +$126.9, against a modelled perfect-fill ceiling of -$104. Yes, the honest number came in above the ceiling. Don't read that as magic fills — it means the ceiling model is mispriced for this sim (it stamps the winning shorts at -$19 when the tick tape paid +$51 and +$78). Trust the real column, ignore the ceiling here.

Split it by side and the story is blunt: the six short rows made +$156 at 67% win, the three long rows lost -$29 at 33%. This wasn't a momentum machine having a great week — it was three well-timed gold shorts on Monday between 05:00 and 09:25 UTC carrying the whole thing, while the longs bled. Exits helped: the winners rode STOP (trailing) exits for +$142; the two RATCHET exits netted -$15.

§23.2 2. Why it won — Goldilocks amplitude

Here's the clean thing, and it's on entry_atr_abs which I have for all 9 rows (full coverage, no gaps). Sort the six entries by how fat the bar was when the sim fired:

  • Bar too quiet (ATR 1.03, the 22:33 long) — lost -$14. Nobody home, no thrust to ride.
  • Bar just right (ATR 1.70-1.73, three entries) — won every time: +$85, +$128, +$17.
  • Bar too fat (ATR 2.39 and 3.41) — lost both: -$56 and -$32. The 3.41 one fired in a shock regime and was the single worst trade of the week.

So it's not a floor and it's not a ceiling — it's a band. This sim wins when there's enough amplitude to carry a thrust but not so much that the bar overruns its own stop. That rhymes with the desk's amplitude-floor thesis, and adds the other half: fat/shock bars run momentum entries over.

The funnel backs this up where it has coverage (only 3 of 9 rows — the early-morning shorts have no funnel rows captured, so this read is 3 points). The one winner sat at rvol 3.93 / atr_pct 0.038 / trending. The two losers with funnel were the dead-tape long (rvol 0.6, atr_pct 0.022) and the shock long (rvol 6.78, atr_pct 0.082). Middle wins, both extremes lose.

Each dot is one of the six entry decisions: horizontal = how fat the bar was, vertical = honest real_pnl. Green wins, red losses. The three winners sit inside the gold band; all three losses fall outside it.

HONEST CAVEAT

This is six entry decisions. A band tuned to exactly where three winners sat is textbook in-sample overfit — a 5/5 vs 0/4 split on n=9 rows will happen by luck more often than you'd like. The direction is trustworthy and matches the desk's amplitude thesis (don't fire momentum into a dead tape, don't fire into a shock bar), but the exact 1.5-2.0 numbers are not. And funnel coverage is only 3/9, so the rvol/atr_pct confirm rests on three points. Don't arm or size this on one week. §24.1 What it's actually doing

This little Dow-micro open-thrust sim made money this week — $28.06 real over 12 fills, 58% winners, and it even beat its own perfect-fill ceiling of $13.67. Good news on the tin. The honest read underneath is thinner: strip out one ratchet ride and the other eleven fills lose money together.

§25.1 1) Did it make money? Yes — $28 honest, and it beat its ceiling

Scoring on the tick-repriced fills, not the sim's optimistic P&L: +$28.06 over 12 trades, 7 winners. The ceiling (perfect-fill fantasy) was only $13.67, so the real fills actually came in ahead — driven by one ratchet exit (trade 11378) the ceiling model priced at a loss but that really banked +$56.38. Cumulative honest P&L, trade by trade (gold dashes = break-even). One jump at trade 2 (the +$56 ratchet), then a slow bleed from a $68 peak down to the $28 close. The whole curve lives above zero on the back of that one step.

§25.2 2) Why — it works when the tape is trending, it bleeds when it chops

Every fill this week was a LONG — it's an open-thrust sim, it buys the pop. What separates the good days from the bad is dead simple and it's exactly what you'd hope for a momentum sim: the regime it fired into.

When the session tape was trending, the thrust followed through: 5 trades, +$47.90, 60% winners. When it was chop, it bought a pop that immediately died and got slapped back into its stop: 7 trades, −$19.84, and even a 57% "win rate" couldn't save it because the losers were fat.

The exit mix says the same thing from the other side. The four hard STOP exits cost it −$81.48 — that's the entire bleed. Everything that didn't get stopped (ratchet, flat-clock, chandelier, session-flat) made +$109.54 between them. When the thrust is real it rides out to a trailing exit; when it's a false pop in chop, it eats the stop. Same entry, two very different tapes.

HONEST CAVEAT

Both buckets are tiny (5 and 7 trades, one week) and the trending bucket itself leans on that single +$56 ratchet. Take it out and trending is +$10.14 over 4 — still green, still beats chop's −$19.84, but nobody should pretend this is settled. It's a lean, not a law.

§25.3 3) The one thing for Monday — stand down in chop, only fire when trending

The cleanest split its own trades offer is session regime, and it matches the sim's whole reason for existing: an open-thrust buyer should only be armed when the tape is actually going somewhere. Gate it to trending regime only and drop every chop fire.

§26 passive_chop: it fades the chop, and the chop keeps going

This one bled the worst on the board. On the honest fills it lost $4,249 over 1,088 trades this week, winning just 17% of the time. And here's the tell: its perfect-fill ceiling was only −$890. So even if every limit had filled at the dream price, this sim still loses money. Real fills just made a bad entry 4.8x worse. This isn't a slippage problem. The entry itself is −EV.

§26.1 What it's actually doing

passive_chop parks a limit order inside quiet, ranging tape and waits to get hit. When it fills, one of two things happens. Either price ticks back a hair and it scratches out a small win, or price fills the limit because it's leaving and keeps right on going until the stop. That second case is the whole story.

Split it by how each trade ended and the asymmetry is brutal. Of the 1,088 orders, 419 never filled at all (free, $0). Of the 669 that did fill, the ratchet exits look lovely on paper — 235 trades, 76% winners, +$563 — but each one only banks about $2.40. Meanwhile the 402 stop-outs lose −$4,789, roughly −$11.91 apiece. It wins small and often, then hands it all back plus rent on the trades that run it over. That's a negative-skew fade, and the exit spine can't save an entry that's fading into moves that keep going.

How it endedTradesReal P&LAvg / tradeWin%
STOP (ran it over)402−$4,788.7−$11.910%
RATCHET (small scratch win)235+$562.9+$2.4076%
FLAT_CLOCK (timed out)32−$23.5−$0.7428%
no_fill (limit never hit)419$0.0——

The picture is the point: the green sliver of ratchet wins is invisible next to the red column of stops. No exit tweak closes that gap because the loser is five times the size of the winner by design.

§26.2 Is there a gate that saves it? I looked. There isn't.

First, the honest caveat: I wanted the entry conditions from the signal funnel, but only 16 of these 1,088 trades have a funnel row (1.5% coverage), so rvol / OFI / book-imbalance splits are off the table this week — not enough data to stand on. So I split on what the sim's own log does record: instrument, hour, and bar width. Every slice bleeds.

  • By hour: there is no green hour. The US afternoon (13–15 UTC) wins 44–53% of the time, far better than the 9% it manages in the European morning — but it's still negative every hour. The best single bucket I could carve, US afternoon excluding bitcoin, was 72 trades at 57% win and still −$264.
  • By bar width: 1,073 of the trades fire at atr_pct under 0.15% — this thing only ever fires in dead-flat tape, so there's no "fat bar" to veto. The bleed is the quiet tape.

The one lever with real money on it is the instrument. MBT (micro bitcoin) is a disaster inside this sim: 365 trades, a 5.2% win rate, −$861. That's a third of the trades for the worst hit rate on the desk. Cutting it is the single biggest defensible save. But look at what's left.

BucketTradesReal P&LWin%
MBT (the veto candidate)365−$860.85.2%
Everything else (kept)723−$3,388.523.4%

Killing MBT saves about $861 in-sample and removes the ugliest instrument — but the remaining book still loses $3,388. There is no gate on rvol, hour, bar size, or symbol that turns this green. The bleed isn't a bad condition you can fence off; it's baked into a −EV fade whose ceiling is already red.

THE ONE THING FOR MONDAY

RETIRE passive_chop Not "tune it" — retire it. Its perfect-fill ceiling is −$890, so the entry loses money before slippage ever gets a vote, and every hour, every bar size, and every instrument bleeds. If you want to keep it breathing on the shadow bench purely to watch, the minimum honest move is to veto MBT (saves ~$861/wk, 5% win rate is indefensible) — but don't kid yourself that it fixes anything; the rest still drops $3,388 a week. This lines up with the reversion autopsy in memory: passive_chop is already flagged for delete because fading quiet tape fades into grinds that keep going. This week is the receipt.

All money on sr.shadow_real_pnl.real_pnl, week filter entry_ts ≥ 2026-07-06. Funnel coverage 16/1088 = 1.5%, so entry-condition splits use the sim's own trade log. One week, in-sample; the MBT save is a real cut, not a curve-fit — but no filter here flips the sign.

§27.1 Why it bleeds: it leans on fast, event-driven names and the stop eats it

The money splits cleanly two ways. First by exit: the RATCHET exit is the only thing working — 87 trades, +$311.8, 62% win. Everything good this sim does, the ratchet banks. But the STOP takes 83 trades to -$935.7 at a 13% win rate. That is the whole loss and then some. When this thing is wrong it is very wrong, and the stop is where it pays for leaning into a move that just keeps going.

Second by symbol, and this is the real tell. Three fast, event-driven names — MBT (micro bitcoin), MNQ (nasdaq) and MCL (crude) — carry 108 trades for -$669.9 at a 20% win rate. The four calmer index-and-gold micros (MGC, MES, MYM, M2K) are 148 trades for -$290 but win 47% — a normal, near-coin-flip book that the ratchet almost keeps afloat. On the fast names the ratchet is dead (+$-11 across 22 trades) and the sim just holds losers into the close: end-of-day exits on those three names alone give back -$286.5.

Put in human words: this is a fade/lean sim. On the calm names it fades a wiggle and the ratchet gets it out clean about half the time. On crude, bitcoin and nasdaq it fades a rip that just runs it over, the stop is too far to save it, and what the stop misses the close finishes off. Its own atr_pct backs this up — the fat-bar names are exactly the losers (MCL 0.096%, MBT 0.071%), the thin ones (MYM 0.019%, MES 0.026%) are the survivors.

Only 7 of 256 trades (2.7%) have a funnel row this week, so I cannot honestly gate this on rvol, OFI, book imbalance or spread — the coverage isn't there. Every split below is built from the trade log itself (symbol, exit, atr_pct), which is fully populated. And this is one in-sample week.

§28.1 1) Did it make money? No — it bled all week

I re-ran the join myself on shadow_real_pnl.real_pnl: 219 trades this week, sum −$891.4, 37% winners, against a perfect-fill ceiling of +$240.9. So the fills didn't kill it — the entries did. The equity curve below never recovers; it just grinds down from zero to about −$900 and stays there.

Cumulative honest P&L, trade by trade in entry order. Gold dashed line is break-even. It touches +$5.64 early and then it's all downhill to −$902.

§28.2 2) Why it loses: small wins, bigger losses, and it stops out constantly

The shape tells the whole story. When it wins it clips a small +$6.9; when it loses it gives back −$10.5. Lose more than half the time at that ratio and you bleed — which is exactly what happened. Break it out by how it exits and you see the engine of the loss:

EXIT MIX

The stop is doing the damage: 73 trades stopped out for −$834 at a 15% win rate. The ratchet is the only thing that works — 71 trades ratcheted out for +$243 at 65% win. The clock exits (FLAT_CLOCK, 29 trades) were roughly flat at −$49. So this is a low-quality entry that, when it's wrong, rides all the way to the stop; when it's right, the ratchet banks a small winner. The wins can't cover the stops.

And it's wrong a lot because it fires into the wrong tape. I matched 175 of the 219 trades to funnel conditions (80% coverage, minute-bucket nearest match) and split every way I could — and nothing is green:

  • Volume: low-rvol trades (rvol<1.0) were 160 of them at −$764; the high-rvol handful (15 trades) still lost −$76. Standing down on thin tape trims it, doesn't fix it.
  • Bar size: quiet bars (atr<0.05) lost −$464, fat bars (atr≥0.05) lost −$376. Both red.
  • Regime: trending −$420, chop −$305, shock −$111. Red, red, red.

There is no condition bucket with real n that this sim makes money in. That's the finding.

§28.3 3) The one thing for Monday: cut its three worst symbols — but it's still red, so retire it

The only split that separates real winners from losers is symbol. Three names carry almost all the loss: MBT (crypto), MCL (crude), and MNQ. Those are the "different-beast" event-driven / high-vol tickers, and this clock strategy gets run over by them — 95 trades, −$675, a 22% win rate. The four calmer index/gold names win about half their trades but still can't turn a profit because the loss size beats the win size.

§29.1 What it's actually doing

Split the week by side and the whole story falls out. The longs — buying strength and holding — roughly paid their way: +$57.7 over 113 trades at 43% win. The shorts are the entire wound: −$685 over 85 trades at 30% win. Same engine, one side works and one side gets steamrolled.

The exit mix says why in plain terms. When a long survives to the 20-minute clock it books green — 52 clock-outs worth +$817. The shorts almost never get there: only 19 clock-winners for +$312, because they keep hitting the stop first. Shorts got stopped 55 times out of 85 (65%) for −$1,377; longs got stopped 50 of 113 (44%). This is a market that ground higher all week, and the sim kept selling into it and getting stopped. Nobody's rewarding a short here — it's fading a tape that just walks away from it.

THE TELL

Shorts get stopped 65% of the time vs 44% for longs, and produce a third as many clock-winners. The sim isn't broken on entries — it's fighting the drift on one side only.

§30.1 1. Did it make money — no, and it's a one-sided wound

I re-ran the join myself on sr.real_pnl, not the sim's optimistic pnl_usd. It's -$542.2. Split it by direction and the story ends: the 30 longs made +$13.5 and won two out of three, the 33 shorts lost -$555.7 and won barely one in three. The longs are already fine. Every dollar of the bleed is short trades.

§30.2 2. Why — it fades a rip above VWAP and the rip keeps going

This is a passive mean-reversion book on gold, and its shorts are all counter-trend fades. Every single one of the 32 shorts I could price fired with gold trading above its VWAP — it sees a pop and sells it, expecting the pop to fade. This week the pops didn't fade. 19 of those shorts hit their stop for -$506.3. That one number — nineteen stopped shorts — is essentially the entire week's loss. The longs, by contrast, exit clean: 17 of them ride the ratchet to +$151.5 in winners, and their stops only cost -$137.9. Same machine, opposite outcome, purely by side.

The condition averages say the same thing a different way. Its winners fire on a slightly fatter, quieter bar (avg atr_pct 0.071, rvol 0.47); its losers fire on a thinner, busier bar pushing away from VWAP (avg atr_pct 0.063, rvol 0.62, vwap_dist +0.037). In plain words: when it sells into an active push away from the mean, the mean doesn't come back and it gets run over.

FUNNEL COVERAGE

Heads up on the join: this sim stamps entry_sid as a session anchor, not the cycle timestamp, so the literal cycle_ts = entry_sid join returns nothing. I matched each trade to the nearest MGC funnel row at or before its entry instead — 62 of 63 trades land within two minutes of a funnel snapshot, so the conditions above are real, not guessed.

The axis that separates winners from losers here is direction. So the Monday change is blunt and honest: on MGC this sim takes longs only — veto the short leg entirely.
