§31.2 Why — it fades rips that just run it over

Look at how the money leaves. The stop-outs are the entire loss: 54 trades exited on STOP for -$1,426, and not one of them was green. Everything that got to manage itself made money — the ratchet booked +$110 over 51 trades at 76% win, the 20-minute session-flat clock booked +$441, and the 7 that hit target made +$333. So when a pullback entry is right, the exit spine harvests it fine. When it's wrong, it's a fader standing in front of a move that keeps going, and it eats the full 1-ATR stop.

The shorts are where that goes wrong. A short "pullback" here is a fade of a rip, and in this tape the rip kept running: short stop-outs alone cost -$732 across 29 trades. The longs take an equally ugly stop bill (-$694 on 25) but they claw it back through ratchet and session-flat wins; the shorts don't. That's the whole difference between a break-even side and a bleeding one.

By symbol the damage tracks size and vol: MGC did the most trading and the most bleeding at -$351 over 102, MNQ -$118 over 32, MES -$73 over 31. No symbol is a hero, but none is the problem the way the short side is.

§31.3 The one thing for Monday

Stand down on the short side. Fire pullback_loose long-only.

It's the single cleanest lever on the sheet. That's an in-sample swing of roughly +$509 for one veto, on a real 84-trade sample, not a five-trade fluke.

And there's a free sharpening on top. Within the longs, amplitude sorts winners from losers cleanly: longs fired on a fat bar (entry ATR ≥ 0.045% of price) made +$203.78 over 31 trades at 68% win, while longs on a thin bar lost -$236.5 over 50 at 40%. Same fat-bar filter does nothing for the shorts (they lose either way), which is exactly why the direction veto has to come first.

§32 pullback_quiet: it only trades when nobody's home, and that's the whole problem

This sim lost real money this week: −$422 over 97 trades, winning just 31% of them, against a perfect-fill ceiling of only +$154.6. So even in the fantasy where every fill is perfect it barely makes a hundred bucks — on the honest tick-repriced fills it bled all week. This is the sim that buys a quiet little pullback and then waits for a bounce that never comes.

§32.1 The bleed never let up

The equity curve is a slide with no shelf. It gives back a little, then a lot, and finishes at its worst point of the week. There is no regime where it turned the corner — it just leaked from the first trade to the last.

Cumulative honest real_pnl, trades in time order. Gold dashed line is break-even. It ends at its low, −$422.

§32.2 Why it loses: it fires into dead tape and the stop pays for it

The name is honest — this thing lives in the quiet. The median relative volume at entry was 0.23, and 75 of the 92 trades I could match to entry conditions fired with rvol below 0.8. That's a pullback into a tape where nobody's trading. The bounce needs buyers to show up, and they don't, so the price just keeps drifting the wrong way until the stop takes it out.

The exit mix tells the same story bluntly: the 35 STOP exits lost −$523 between them and won exactly zero of them. Everything good this sim did — the 7 TARGET hits at +$111.5, the 18 ratchets near flat — got swamped by that one bucket. When the entry has no follow-through, the stop is just a scheduled donation.

Two more things it's doing wrong: it's worse fat than thin (bars with atr_pct at or above 0.05 bled −$287 over 36 trades at ~10% win — fading a fast bar in a thin book is how you get run over), and the short side is the heavier bleeder (−$272 over 40 shorts vs −$150 over 57 longs). Funnel coverage for this read was 92 of 97 trades matched within 180 seconds, so this is the near-whole sample, not a corner of it.

WHAT IT'S ACTUALLY DOING

Buying a small dip when the tape is empty and hoping for a bounce. In empty tape there's no one to provide the bounce, so the dip keeps going and the stop eats it. Its own premise — "trade the quiet" — is the bug.

§32.3 The one thing for Monday: don't fire unless someone's actually in the tape

The single cleanest cut is a relative-volume floor. Split its own trades on rvol ≥ 0.8 and the bleed almost entirely lives on one side:

Bucketnreal_pnlwin%
rvol ≥ 0.8 (someone's home) — KEEP17−$18.953%
rvol < 0.8 (dead tape) — VETO75−$390.927%

Vetoing the dead-tape trades removes −$390.9 of in-sample bleed and takes the win rate from 27% to 53%. That's the gate: only fire when rvol ≥ 0.8 and it stops being a money furnace.

BE HONEST ABOUT WHAT THIS GATE IS

This isn't a tune that flips it green. Even gated, the surviving 17 trades are still slightly red at −$18.9 — call it break-even. And the gate throws away 82% of the sim's trades, because "quiet" is what it was built to do. So this is really the split saying the same thing the whole section says: the premise is broken. One week, in-sample, and the honest read is "trade 5× less and land near zero." A relative-volume floor stops the bleeding; it does not make this pay.

Verdict: GATE, on probation to retire. Put the rvol ≥ 0.8 floor on it Monday so it stops bleeding, but the honest truth is a pullback sim that only trades empty tape has nothing to buy. If gated-and-near-flat is the best it can do, it comes off the book next week. Removing ~$391/wk of in-sample bleed (≈£307) is the whole prize here, and it's a prize made of not losing, not of winning.

§33 MCL "Short With Strength" — It Shorts Into A Tape That's Still Trending, And The Trend Runs It Over

This one bled this week. It shorted crude 295 times and lost $341 on the honest fills, winning barely a third of the time. And here's the sting: on perfect fills it would have made $958. The gap isn't the strategy being wrong everywhere — it's one specific mistake it makes over and over, and the fix is a single stand-down rule.

§33.1 What it's actually doing — shorting strength and getting steamrolled

The name is honest: it sells into a strong move. When the tape is choppy or shocked, that works — it caught +$42 across 76 chop trades and +$36 across 15 shock trades. But crude spent most of this week trending, and that's where the whole loss lives. In the trending bucket it took 122 trades, won only 26% of them, and lost $407. It fades a rip that just keeps running, the stop hits, and it does it again an hour later.

You can see it in the exits. The 135 stop-outs never won once and cost $1,447. Everything green came from the exits that let a good short breathe — CHANDELIER made +$537, RATCHET +$419, the 20-minute flat-clock +$159. So the exit spine is fine. The problem is upstream: it's opening shorts into up-trends that were never going to pay.

The entry conditions back this up. On the 213 trades I could match to a live signal-funnel row, the losers came in on higher relative volume (rvol 0.83 vs 0.61 on winners) and further below VWAP (−0.15% vs −0.09%) — i.e. it's chasing a move that's already extended and still accelerating, then shorting the front of it.

§34 The MBT Euphoria-Fade: Shorting Into A Freight Train

This sim shorts bitcoin when it looks euphoric, betting the rip is exhausted. This week the rip wasn't exhausted. It bled $-312 over 50 trades and won just 3 of them. And here's the part that kills any hope of a tune: even with perfect fills it still loses $-240. This isn't a slippage problem. The signal itself is wrong.

§34.1 1. The money — I re-ran it myself

Joined the sim's trades to the honest tick-repriced fills for the week from Monday 6 July. Fifty shorts, all of them fading a move up in MBT. Total honest damage $-312.00 against a perfect-fill fantasy of $-239.75. So real fills cost an extra $72 on top of a signal that was already losing. When the fantasy version of your strategy loses money, the fill isn't the problem.

Cumulative honest P&L, trade by trade. Gold dashed line is breakeven. It never comes back up.

§34.2 2. Why — it fades a rip that just runs it over

The behaviour is simple and brutal. The sim sees MBT spike, calls it euphoria, and sells short. Then the spike keeps going. 41 of the 50 trades hit their stop, and every single stopped trade was a loser, for $-306.5 — that's basically the whole week's bleed right there. The only trades that didn't get run over were the 9 that survived to the flat-clock cut, and those netted just $-5.5 with the sim's only 3 winners in them. So the picture is: when the fade isn't immediately steamrolled it mean-reverts fine by the clock, but 8 times out of 10 the rip is a real trend and it plows straight through the stop.

The holds tell the same story — most losers are dead inside 60-72 seconds. It's short, it's wrong, it's stopped, over and over. The few funnel rows I could see (more on that below) show it firing into "trending" tape, which is exactly the reversion-fades-a-trend failure we already booked on the reversion book: it never bounds how strong the move it's fading is, so it sells into grinds that don't stop.

THE HONEST CAVEAT

Only 3 of 50 trades have a matching entry-conditions row in the signal funnel — 6% coverage. That means I cannot honestly hand you an rvol or atr_pct or book-imbalance gate for this sim this week; there simply aren't enough rows to split on. Anyone who shows you a microstructure filter on this book right now is fitting three data points. The capture gap is the fix to chase separately; it doesn't rescue the money.

The exit isn't the villain either: the flat-clock trades were nearly breakeven, so a better exit just gets you to zero, not to profit.

VERDICT

§35 Strength-Ceiling Pullback: the shorts are the disease, thin fills are the fever

This one bled. On the honest tick-repriced fills, tw_strength_ceiling_pb lost $266.9 over 36 trades this week, winning just 42%. §35.1 Why it bled: two different diseases wearing one coat

Split it by side and the picture snaps into focus. The 22 shorts lost $207.8 — and their ceiling was -$46.5, so even with perfect fills the shorts lose money. That is a signal problem: this thing shorts a pullback into strength and the strength just keeps running it over. All three MNQ shorts lost (0-for-3, -$101.9). The single worst trade was an MBT short that got carried to the close for -$62.

The 14 longs are a different animal. They lost $59.1 on the honest fills — but their ceiling was +$16.1. The long signal was actually fine, a small winner on paper. Then honest fills ate $75 and turned it red. That is the "nobody's home" tax — on the micros this book trades, the fill you get in a thin tape is the one you didn't want. Look at the MES short that should have banked $76.5 at the ceiling and came home at +$0.9 — the whole move, gone to fills.

The exit mix confirms it: the STOP bucket is the graveyard — 12 stop-outs, 17% wins, -$183.2. Those are entries that were simply wrong-way; the exit did its job cutting them. The RATCHET and FLAT_CLOCK exits were both slightly green (+$7.3 and +$6.6), because those are the trades that went the right way from the start. This sim doesn't have an exit problem. It has an entry problem, and the entry problem is mostly the shorts.

FILLS vs SIGNAL, BY SIDE

Shorts: real -$207.8 vs ceiling -$46.5 — the signal itself loses. Longs: real -$59.1 vs ceiling +$16.1 — the signal is fine, the fills kill it. One coat, two diseases.

§35.2 The one thing for Monday: stand down the shorts

There is no clever microstructure gate here — the signal funnel only caught 2 of the 36 trades this week, so rvol / book / session vetoes have nothing to stand on. The honest lever is the one that shows up on every trade: side. Veto the short side of this sim and you remove 78% of the bleed.

Bucketnreal P&Lwin%ceiling
SHORTS (veto these)22-$207.845%-$46.5
LONGS (keep, on a leash)14-$59.136%+$16.1
In-sample swing from the veto—+$207.8——

Be straight about what this does and doesn't do. Killing the shorts is a +$207.8 in-sample swing, and it's the right call — even perfect fills couldn't save them. But it does not flip the sim green: the longs still come home at -$59.1, because their disease is fills, not direction. And this is one week, 36 trades — a side veto on a single week mostly just means "trade less," and here it means "trade a lot less."

So the verdict is KILL the short side LONGS on watch. The long signal has a real, if tiny, paper edge (+$16 ceiling) that a fatter, less-thin tape might let through — but until it can beat its own fills it stays observe-only. If the longs are still negative next week, the whole sim goes in the departure lounge. A signal whose best-case perfect-fill week is -$30 is not one to fall in love with.

The strategy is a trend-continuation pullback, and this week the pullbacks weren't pullbacks — they were reversals.

The other two exited on the RATCHET and are worth staring at, because they're the memo "shadow sim understates losses" made flesh. Trade 17139's ceiling says +$110.38; the tick-repriced fill says -$27.12. That's a $137 gap between the dream and the money. Trade 15997: ceiling +$11.00, real -$25.12. In the sim's fantasy those two made $121 together; on real MGC fills they lost $52. Gold ticks that never actually printed at your order.

Conditions at entry (nearest funnel row within 2s of each fire — 7/7 covered): it fired into thin, low-participation tape. Five of seven had rvol under 1.0, three of them under 0.5 (0.32, 0.44, 0.50). A "strong pullback only" strategy is supposed to demand a strong move to pull back from — instead it took dead-tape entries. And the two fattest bars took the two worst beatings.

Vetoing the fat bars doesn't uncover an edge — it just makes a losing strategy lose slightly less slowly. That's not a gate, that's trading less of a bad thing.

Same story on every other axis: chop entries lost -$94.68 over 4, trending lost -$103.36 over 3 — both red. High rvol (>=1.0) lost -$46.24 over 2, low rvol lost -$151.60 over 5 — both red. There is no bucket, on any condition, where this sim made money this week. Seven trades is thin and it's one week, so I won't over-claim a "structural flaw" from n=7 — but a strategy that goes 0-for-7 with its two best trades being fill mirages has earned a rest, not a tweak.

This dip-buyer took 131 shots this week and lost money on the honest fills — down $184.5 against a perfect-fill fantasy of $1,200. But that headline hides the real story: it's a good sim wearing two bad markets. On MNQ it made money and beat nobody's-fault trouble. On gold and the S&P micro it bled. The fix isn't a knob — it's dropping two symbols.

§39.2 2) Why — gold's fills eat the edge, MES has no edge to eat

Look at the ceiling column, because it tells you where the edge even exists. On MES the perfect-fill ceiling is only $31.5 — even if you filled every trade at the ideal price, this sim barely makes lunch money on the S&P micro. There is no edge there to rescue. That's the widest fantasy-to-reality gap on the desk: the dip you want to buy in gold is the one nobody's selling you at your price, and the fill you actually get is the bad one.

The exit mix confirms it's a fill/stop problem, not a target problem. When dip_loose is right it books nicely — TARGET exits made +$472 over 6 trades, session-flats and ratchets another +$361.5 combined. On MNQ the same stop only cost -$318.1 and the winners more than paid for it.

IN PLAIN WORDS

dip_loose buys a dip and waits for the bounce. On MNQ the bounce shows up often enough and the fills are clean enough that it pays. On gold the setup is fine but the tape is thin at the price you need — you buy the dip and the dip keeps going, then the stop fills worse than modelled. On MES there was never enough edge to begin with.

I checked the usual within-symbol gates — the funnel matched 117 of 131 trades on nearest cycle timestamp (the documented entry_sid join is a session bucket, so I matched on entry_ts to ~1 second). Nothing clean separated winners from losers inside a symbol: fat bars vs thin were both negative (-$86.0 vs -$122.2), and book imbalance barely moved the needle. The one axis that actually splits this sim is which contract it's trading.

You cannot gate your way out of that — even if every fill were perfect this thing barely clears zero, and the fills are never perfect.

§43 MGC Midband Pullback: It Buys Into Selling and Gets Run Over

This one bled. On the honest tick-repriced fills it lost $-107.7 across 31 trades this week, won only 45% of them, and left a perfect-fill ceiling of $249.1 on the table. So the money was there to be had — the fills and the bad entries ate it.

$-107.7
honest real_pnl, 31 trades
45%
win rate
$249.1
perfect-fill ceiling (fantasy)

§43.1 Why it bled: the winners trail out, the losers get stopped

Split by how each trade ended and the story is stark. The trades that got to trail a ratchet made money — 18 RATCHET exits, +$110.8, 61% winners. The trades that hit the stop are the whole loss and then some — 12 STOP exits, -$221.4, and only 17% of those were green. One lone flat-clock exit chipped in $2.9. There is nothing wrong with the exit here. The stops are firing because the entries are bad.

WHAT IT'S ACTUALLY DOING

It's a pullback strategy — it waits for price to pull back to a mid-band and buys the dip (or sells the rip). When it works, the pullback holds and it trails a ratchet up. When it fails, the pullback isn't a pullback at all — it's the start of a real move that keeps going, and it walks straight into a stop. It's fading a move that isn't done.

The entry conditions tell you exactly which is which. Matching each trade to the signal funnel at its entry moment (28 of 31 matched — the sim's entry_sid is the session id, not the cycle timestamp, so I matched the nearest funnel row within 90s), the winners and losers separate cleanly on order flow:

Bucketnreal_pnlavg OFIavg rvolavg vwap-dist
Winners12+$204.6+19.70.480.002
Losers16-$315.9-4.80.720.015

Read that in plain words. The winners came in with order flow on their side (OFI positive, +19.7), in a quieter tape (rvol 0.48), and right on the band (barely off vwap). The losers faded into net selling (OFI negative), in a hotter tape (rvol 0.72), reaching further from vwap to do it. It's buying the dip while the dip is still being sold — and the dip keeps going.

§43.2 The one thing for Monday: don't fade into the order flow

The single condition that best separates its own winners from its losers this week is order-flow imbalance. Gate it: only fire when OFI > 0 — stand down when net flow is against the pullback.

OFI gatenreal_pnlwin%
KEEP — OFI > 0 (flow with you)16-$11.950%
DROP — OFI ≤ 0 (fading into selling)12-$99.433%

The dropped bucket is nearly the entire week's loss: those 12 trades cost -$99.4 at a 33% hit rate. Cut them and what's left is 16 trades at about breakeven (-$11.9, 50% win). So in-sample the gate swings roughly +$99 for the week versus running the sim as-is.

Cumulative honest P&L, trade by trade (gold dashes = breakeven). It never gets above the line after trade 5 and grinds to a -$195 low before the late ratchets claw some back. That mid-week trench is the OFI-negative fades.

BE HONEST ABOUT IT

This is one week and thin — 12 trades in the drop bucket, 16 kept, and even the "good" bucket is only breakeven, not green. The gate doesn't make this sim pay; it stops it bleeding. That's mostly "trade less on the days flow is against you." The mechanism is sound — a pullback entry that fires while the tape is selling into you deserves to be vetoed — but I'd want another week or two before calling it an edge. Long and short bled about evenly (LONG -$62.9, SHORT -$44.8), so this isn't a one-sided problem.

GATE: OFI > 0 Add the order-flow veto and re-observe. If it holds up out-of-sample it's a keep; if the breakeven doesn't turn green in two weeks, retire it — a midband fader that only manages breakeven isn't worth the margin.

GATE IT — one filter fixes it · $-102 this week on honest fills.
On honest fills this MES sim lost $101.50 over 21 trades (43% win) versus a +$48.10 ceiling, and every dollar of it is 8 stop-outs on fat bars; Monday move is tighten its ATR ceiling to veto entries at atr_pct >= 0.020.

§44 tw_mes_atr_ceiling_003 — the ceiling's set too high, so it keeps buying the fat bars that stop it out

This one bled this week. On the honest tick-repriced fills it lost $101.50 across 21 trades, won just 43% of them, and it did that while its perfect-fill ceiling said it should have made +$48.10. So this isn't slippage eating a good book — the entries themselves are wrong. And they're wrong in one very specific, very fixable way.

§44.1 Where the money actually went

The whole loss is the stops. Nothing subtle here — split it by how each trade left and the picture is stark. Every trade that got stopped out lost, and together they are more than the entire week's damage. The trades that got to ratchet out, or that just ran to the session flatten, were fine.

How it exitednreal_pnlwin%
STOP8-$111.700%
SESSION_FLAT6+$0.3050%

So the question becomes simple: what's different about the trades that get stopped? And the answer is sitting right in the name of the sim. It's an ATR ceiling sim — it's supposed to stand down when the bar gets fat. Its ceiling is just set too loose, and the fat bars are walking straight through it.

§44.2 Why — thin tape it ratchets out green, fat tape it gets run over

Funnel conditions cover 19 of the 21 trades. Line up each entry's bar-size (atr_pct) against what it made and the split is clean: the losers sit on the fat bars to the right, the winners sit on the thin bars to the left. When the bar is calm this thing quietly grabs its ratchet and books a small win. When the bar is wide, price has enough room to swing through its stop before the ratchet ever arms — and it eats a full stop-out.

Each dot is one trade: horizontal = bar-size at entry (atr_pct), vertical = honest real_pnl, green above the dotted $0 line, red below. The deep losses all live to the right of the gold line.

The regime tag doesn't sort it — losers show up in both "chop" and "trending" — and rvol doesn't sort it either (losers ran from 0.11 to 0.87). It's bar-size, plain and simple. The three worst trades of the week were shorts and longs entered at atr_pct 0.028, 0.027 and 0.024, and they lost $19.10, $17.90 and $16.60. The winners almost all came in under 0.017.

§44.3 The one thing for Monday — bring the ceiling down to atr_pct 0.020

Split its own trades at an atr_pct ceiling of 0.020 and it separates the book almost perfectly. Everything at or above 0.020 is a loss factory; everything below is roughly break-even with a real win rate.

Bucketnreal_pnlwin%avg/trade
FAT — atr_pct >= 0.020 (veto these)9-$92.1022%-$10.20
THIN — atr_pct < 0.020 (keep)12-$9.4058%-$0.80

GATE: stand down when atr_pct >= 0.020 Vetoing the nine fat-bar entries removes -$92.10 of loss in-sample and takes the week from -$101.50 to about -$9.40. The kept book wins 58% and averages a hair under zero per trade.

BE HONEST

This gate stops the bleed; it does not mint money. The kept 12 trades still net -$9.40 — flat, not green — so the honest read is "the ceiling is set too high, tighten it," not "flip a switch and print." It's one week and thin-n (nine vetoed, twelve kept), and any single bar-size filter partly just means trade less. But the mechanism is the sim's own stated purpose failing: it's an ATR-ceiling sim whose ceiling let the fat bars through. Bringing the ceiling from wherever it sits down to ~0.020 is a tightening of its existing knob, not a new bolt-on, which is exactly the kind of change that tends to hold up out of sample. Watch it next week; if the thin book stays break-even-plus, this earns its keep.

§45 passive_chop_calm: it fades the quiet, but the quiet is overnight and the fills are ugly

This one bled again. On the honest tick-repriced fills it lost $95 over 46 trades this week, winning just 26% of the time. Its perfect-fill ceiling was $-161.5, so the fills didn't rob it — the entries did. It is a passive reversion sim that fades calm chop, and this week the calm it found was the overnight tape, where a fade that gets picked off gets picked off hard.

§45.1 What it's actually doing

Look at how it exits and the story writes itself. When the tape stays genuinely calm and the position just times out, it's fine — the 20-minute FLAT_CLOCK exits went +$13.9 over 15 trades at 53% win. The damage is the other two doors: it got stopped 17 times for -$69.7 (18% win) and dragged to the close 14 times for -$39.3 (7% win). That is the signature of a reversion book fading a move that keeps going: sit in the chop and you scratch out, but the moment price actually picks a direction it runs your fade over.

And it happens overnight. One trade tells the whole tale — a MGC short at 00:38 UTC that got stopped for -$43.6 by itself, nearly half the week's loss in a single thin-tape gold fade. By symbol it's Bitcoin that's the sinkhole: MBT lost $45 over 15 trades at 7% win, a coin flip that never landed. The daytime, real-session names (MES, MYM, MNQ, M2K) are all roughly flat-to-green.

Only 4 of 46 trades matched a fut_signal_funnel row, and all 4 were losers, so I can't honestly split this sim on rvol / atr_pct / book. Also worth knowing: 22 of 46 trades never got a fill status (real_pnl is NULL) — the live money is really 24 filled trades. So the gate below is built on the clocks and the tape hours I can trust, not on microstructure I can't.

Be honest with yourself about what this is, though: the gate mostly works by trading 7 times instead of 46. It's a "stand down" more than a "make money" — the surviving US bucket is only 7 trades and +$2.6 is a scratch, not an edge. This is one week and in-sample. §47.1 Did it make money? No — and the shape tells you why

It bled all week, in a straight line down. Here's the honest equity curve, all 39 trades in order. No bad day did the damage — it was a steady drip.

Gold dashed line = break-even. Every trade drags it lower; the curve never gets back above zero after trade three.

§47.2 Why it bleeds: small winners, full-size stops

The engine underneath is broken in a familiar way. The winners get ratcheted out for lunch money and the losers ride all the way to the stop. Average winner: +$3.24. Average loser: -$6.48. Losers are twice the size of winners, so 46% win isn't nearly enough to stay alive.

The ratchet and clock exits are actually green. The entire $77 hole is the 16 stop-outs, and only 3 of those ever showed a profit. So the question isn't the exit — it's "why does it keep buying drives that immediately reverse and stop out?"

§47.3 The answer: it chases drives while the tape is sagging

I pulled the entry conditions from the signal funnel — 34 of the 39 trades matched a funnel snapshot within five minutes (87% coverage; 5 had no row). The cleanest tell by a mile is where price sits relative to the session VWAP at entry. VWAP is the day's centre of gravity. Above it, the tape has an up-posture; below it, it's leaking.

THE TELL

When it fired with price at or above VWAP, it made +$7.2 over 15 trades at 60% win. When it fired below VWAP, it lost -$81.3 over 19 trades at 32% win. Same story on the killer stops: below-VWAP stop-outs lost -$72.2 (10 trades); above-VWAP stops only -$14.2 (2 trades).

In plain words: this is a momentum-continuation sim, but below VWAP the momentum it's trying to ride is down, so its upward drive-chases fail and get run over. It's also firing in thin tape — average rvol around 0.4-0.5, well under a normal bar — so there's often no real drive there to continue in the first place.

§47.4 The one thing for Monday

GATE: only fire when vwap_dist_pct >= 0

BucketTradesReal P&LWin%
At/above VWAP (keep)15+$7.260%
Below VWAP (stand down)19-$81.332%

Stand down when price is below the session VWAP. In-sample that turns the matched book from -$74 into +$7 — roughly an $81 swing — and lifts the win rate from 32% to 60%. It works because it deletes exactly the trades that were producing the fatal stop-outs.

HONEST CAVEAT

This is one week, in-sample, and it's thin: the gate keeps only 15 of 34 matched trades, so mostly it just means trade a lot less. It also nearly kills the shorts — they cluster right at/below VWAP — so the sim effectively becomes a long-only "up-posture drive" filter. I wouldn't size it up on this. Arm it observe-only, and re-check next week that above-VWAP drives still pay before believing the $7 is edge and not just survivorship.

Verdict: Don't retire it yet — there's a real, sensible posture filter here (chase drives with the tape, not against it) that switches the bleed off. Gate it on VWAP posture, keep it in the shadow, and prove it holds up out-of-sample before it earns anything more.

§51.1 Did it make money? No — the stops give back everything the ratchet earns

§51.2 Why — it buys the dip when it wins, and chases the rip when it loses

I matched each trade back to the entry-condition funnel (M2K rows within 90 seconds; 43 of 66 trades matched, 65% coverage — the rest have no funnel row, so read the split as directional, not gospel). The winners and losers separate on one thing: which side of VWAP the trade fired on.

Winners fire when price sits below VWAP on ordinary tape (average rvol 1.06) — it's buying the dip and fading the stretch, which is exactly what a short-term VWAP-reversion sim is supposed to do. Losers fire when price is already above VWAP on hotter tape (average rvol 1.29) — that's chasing strength that's already run, and the stop catches the knife. The damage is almost entirely one shape: longs taken above VWAP lost $45.8 over 19 trades at 26% wins. That single wrong-side-of-the-line bucket is the whole bleed.

THE MONDAY MOVE

Veto any entry where vwap_dist_pct ≥ 0 — don't buy above VWAP, don't take the trade on the wrong side of the line. On the 43 covered trades that turns −$19.7 into +$21.9, a ~$42 in-sample swing, and lifts win rate from 44% to 59%. Applied to the full book it removes the −$41.5 chasing bucket outright, taking the honest week from breakeven to roughly +$41.

GATE: vwap_dist < 0 only ratchet exit is fine, keep it

The honest caveats. This is one week and only 65% of trades carry a funnel row, so the exact dollars are soft. And a sign-of-VWAP gate mostly means trade less — it keeps 22 trades and bins 21, roughly half the book. But this isn't a random threshold fished from noise: it's the sim's own reversion thesis. It's meant to fade stretch back to VWAP, and the money proves it only works when it enters on the reversion side. The trades above VWAP aren't reversion at all — they're the sim forgetting what it is. Gate that out and it's a small, real, green edge instead of a wash. Worth arming as an observe-only veto and re-checking coverage next week before it earns size.

§52 The Small Fry — too thin to judge, worth a glance

Two sims fired so few times this week there's nothing to prove either way. Here's what they are and whether to keep an eye on them. Don't read money into five trades.

-$8.48
M2K absorption · 4 trades
-$12.00
MBT spike-fade · 1 trade

§52.1 M2K absorption-only WATCH

This one waits for the book to soak up selling and then buys the little Russell. It fired four times this week and lost $8.48 net on honest fills. Two got stopped for a combined $13.74, one ratcheted out for a scratch, and one rode the flat-clock to a $5.88 win. That's not a signal, that's a coin landing tails three times out of four. The fills were clean — filled every time, no phantom slippage story here, real money tracked the ceiling within a dollar or two. Leave it running and look again when it has twenty trades. Four tells us nothing.

§52.2 MBT spike-fade IGNORE

One trade all week. It faded a bitcoin spike, held for four minutes, and got carried out at the EOD flatten for -$12. The tell isn't the loss, it's the fill: the perfect-fill ceiling said this was only a -$4 miss, and the honest tick-repriced fill was three times worse at -$12. That's MBT being MBT — thin book, wide spread, nobody home to give you the price you wanted. One trade can't earn a verdict, but everything we already know about micro-bitcoin says the fill will keep eating any edge this thing thinks it has. Ignore it until it either fires enough to argue with or we fix the venue.

THE ONE THING

Nothing to change on Monday. Both are below the threshold where a filter means anything — any "fix" on n=4 or n=1 is just noise-fitting. Let them accumulate. M2K is worth the disk space; MBT's fill problem is the real story and it's structural, not a gate you can tune.

Every figure is from the shadow desk's own honest, tick-repriced fills (shadow_real.db :: real_pnl) for the week (Mon 6 -> Fri 10 Jul 2026) — never the sims' own optimistic numbers, which understate losses. One agent dissected each sim; independent skeptics re-ran every Monday refinement worth banking, and their verdict is stamped on each section. The live desk is settled and is not the subject here.
